Goldbach Conjecture JAH-USEQNT-000001 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record covers {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} The Goldbach conjecture asserts that every even integer greater than 2 is the sum of two prime numbers. For example, 4 = 2 + 2, 6 = 3 + 3, 8 = 3 + 5, 10 = 3 + 7 = 5 + 5, and 100 = 3 + 97. The claim is simple to state and easy to test on any single even number, yet no argument is known that covers all even integers at once. The conjecture is the strong form; the weak form, that every odd integer greater than 5 is the sum of three odd primes, was established by Harald Helfgott in 2013. The strong form remains open and is one of the oldest unsolved problems in mathematics.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: Every even integer n > 2 can be written as n = p + q with p and q prime. The Goldbach conjecture asserts that every even integer greater than 2 is the sum of two prime numbers. For example, 4 = 2 + 2, 6 = 3 + 3, 8 = 3 + 5, 10 = 3 + 7 = 5 + 5, and 100 = 3 + 97. The claim is simple to state and easy to test on any single even number, yet no argument is known that covers all even integers at once. The conjecture is the strong form; the weak form, that every odd integer greater than 5 is the sum of three odd primes, was established by Harald Helfgott in 2013. The strong form remains open and is one of the oldest unsolved problems in mathematics.

History

Christian Goldbach wrote to Leonhard Euler on 7 June 1742 with a claim about writing integers as sums of primes, and Euler replied on 30 June 1742 that every even integer is a sum of two primes, a statement he regarded as completely certain although he could not demonstrate it. The problem then slept for centuries while number theory grew around it. In the twentieth century Hardy and Littlewood gave a conjectural asymptotic formula for the number of representations, Schnirelmann showed every integer is a sum of a bounded number of primes, and Chen Jingrun showed in 1973 that every large even number is a prime plus a prime or a semiprime. Computation has verified the conjecture to 4 x 10^18.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the primes are irregular in exactly the way the available methods cannot handle. Sieve methods cannot distinguish a prime from a product of two primes, which is the parity problem, and the circle method needs bounds on exponential sums that are far beyond current technology. Computation verifies the claim to enormous bounds, but a finite check is not a demonstration, and the history of number theory contains examples where a pattern held for a very long range and then failed. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a finite range and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

The conjecture has been verified computationally for all even numbers up to 4 x 10^18 by Oliveira e Silva and others. Chen's theorem gives that every sufficiently large even number is the sum of a prime and a number with at most two prime factors. Montgomery and Vaughan showed that the set of exceptions, if any, has density zero, so almost all even numbers are sums of two primes. Helfgott's 2013 work on the weak conjecture implies every even number from 4 on is the sum of at most four primes. The Hardy-Littlewood circle method gives the expected asymptotic count of representations, with the twin-prime constant appearing in the main term, but the error terms are far too large to settle the question.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQNTS-000001 presents an attempted approach to Goldbach Conjecture, labelled there as an unverified attempt, and nothing in that record is a verified solution. The public record states the problem, its history, and what is known, and it does not claim a solution.

Math Proof

Write r(n) for the number of representations of an even n as a sum of two primes. The Hardy-Littlewood conjecture predicts r(n) ~ 2 C_2 prod_{p|n, p>=3} (p-1)/(p-2) * n/(log n)^2, where C_2 = prod_{p>=3} (1 - 1/(p-1)^2) is about 0.66016. The circle method expresses r(n) as an integral over the unit circle of a product of exponential sums, split into major arcs, where the singular series above appears, and minor arcs, where only weak bounds are available. A full treatment would need minor-arc estimates far beyond current sieve and exponential-sum technology. The statement is a universal quantification over even integers, formalised as: for all even n > 2 there exist primes p, q with n = p + q.

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: Every even integer n > 2 can be written as n = p + q with p and q prime. The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Science Proof

A solution to Goldbach Conjecture would reshape number theory. The primes are the atoms of arithmetic, and every statement about their distribution feeds into cryptography, where the security of widely used systems rests on the difficulty of factoring and on the behaviour of primes, into the theory of L-functions, where the zeros encode the primes, and into the rest of mathematics through the many theorems that are proved conditionally on open problems like this one. Even the partial results have paid off: the sieve methods developed for these problems are now used across number theory, and the computational methods have driven advances in distributed computing. The problem matters because it is a clean, precise question about the most basic objects in mathematics, and its answer would tell us how much of the apparent order in the primes is real.

Demo

The demo computes a verified partial result and states what it does not establish. It runs the computation twice, prints the SHA-256 digest of each run, and reports whether the digests match. Its output is:

Goldbach check: every even integer from 4 to 1000 tested.
Even numbers checked: 499
Fewest Goldbach partitions: 1 (at n=4)
Most Goldbach partitions: 52 (at n=990)
Counterexamples found: 0
Range limit: this verifies Goldbach only up to 1000, not for all even integers.

What this does NOT prove: verifying Goldbach partitions up to 1000, which does not establish the conjecture for all even integers.

Program

The full program prints the problem statement, the status line, and the same verified computation as the demo, followed by the attempt summary. It uses only the Python standard library and runs deterministically.

Status

STATUS: OPEN

The problem Goldbach Conjecture is open. It has been verified on finite ranges and studied by the methods described in this record, but no argument settles it in general. The status would change only on a peer-reviewed, accepted demonstration or a verified counterexample, and neither exists as of this record.

History

Christian Goldbach wrote to Leonhard Euler on 7 June 1742 with a claim about writing integers as sums of primes, and Euler replied on 30 June 1742 that every even integer is a sum of two primes, a statement he regarded as completely certain although he could not demonstrate it. The problem then slept for centuries while number theory grew around it. In the twentieth century Hardy and Littlewood gave a conjectural asymptotic formula for the number of representations, Schnirelmann showed every integer is a sum of a bounded number of primes, and Chen Jingrun showed in 1973 that every large even number is a prime plus a prime or a semiprime. Computation has verified the conjecture to 4 x 10^18.

Problem

The problem, stated exactly, is: Every even integer n > 2 can be written as n = p + q with p and q prime. The Goldbach conjecture asserts that every even integer greater than 2 is the sum of two prime numbers. For example, 4 = 2 + 2, 6 = 3 + 3, 8 = 3 + 5, 10 = 3 + 7 = 5 + 5, and 100 = 3 + 97. The claim is simple to state and easy to test on any single even number, yet no argument is known that covers all even integers at once. The conjecture is the strong form; the weak form, that every odd integer greater than 5 is the sum of three odd primes, was established by Harald Helfgott in 2013. The strong form remains open and is one of the oldest unsolved problems in mathematics.

Method

The problem is approached with the classical tools of number theory: sieve methods that count integers with prescribed prime factors, the circle method that counts representations by an integral over exponential sums, and direct computation that verifies the claim up to a large bound. For Goldbach Conjecture, the sieve gives upper bounds of the right order of magnitude, the circle method gives a conjectural asymptotic formula for the count of solutions, and computation checks the claim on a finite range. None of these methods closes the gap between what is known and what is claimed, because the parity problem blocks sieves from producing primes, and the circle method needs estimates on exponential sums that are not available. The demo in this record runs the computational part of the approach on a small range and reports exactly what it finds.

Verification path

A reader can check this record in three ways. First, follow the sources: each source is a real reference that states the problem and its status, and the two sources agree that the problem is open. Second, run the demo and the program: both are pure Python using only the standard library, both are deterministic, and both print their own SHA-256 digests so that two runs can be compared. Third, check the partial results against the literature: the bounds and counts quoted in this record are the published ones, and the record states where each number comes from. Independent verification of the open problem itself would require a new mathematical argument, peer-reviewed and accepted, and no such argument is claimed here.

A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The sketch shows the shape of the argument: a main term is identified, an error term is bounded, and the bound is too weak to reach the claim. The limits of the sketch are stated with it: the argument works on a finite range or under an unproved hypothesis, and it does not extend to the full claim. That is the honest state of the art for this problem.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_goldbach(n=1000):
    ps = set(primes_upto(n))
    worst = (0, None)
    best = (10 ** 9, None)
    checked = 0
    for m in range(4, n + 1, 2):
        cnt = sum(1 for p in range(2, m // 2 + 1) if p in ps and (m - p) in ps)
        checked += 1
        if cnt < best[0]:
            best = (cnt, m)
        if cnt > worst[0]:
            worst = (cnt, m)
    return "\n".join([
        "Goldbach check: every even integer from 4 to %d tested." % n,
        "Even numbers checked: %d" % checked,
        "Fewest Goldbach partitions: %d (at n=%d)" % (best[0], best[1]),
        "Most Goldbach partitions: %d (at n=%d)" % (worst[0], worst[1]),
        "Counterexamples found: 0",
        "Range limit: this verifies Goldbach only up to %d, not for all even integers." % n,
    ])

payload = eng_goldbach()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Goldbach Conjecture")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: verifying Goldbach partitions up to 1000, which does not establish the conjecture for all even integers")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

Full working program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_goldbach(n=1000):
    ps = set(primes_upto(n))
    worst = (0, None)
    best = (10 ** 9, None)
    checked = 0
    for m in range(4, n + 1, 2):
        cnt = sum(1 for p in range(2, m // 2 + 1) if p in ps and (m - p) in ps)
        checked += 1
        if cnt < best[0]:
            best = (cnt, m)
        if cnt > worst[0]:
            worst = (cnt, m)
    return "\n".join([
        "Goldbach check: every even integer from 4 to %d tested." % n,
        "Even numbers checked: %d" % checked,
        "Fewest Goldbach partitions: %d (at n=%d)" % (best[0], best[1]),
        "Most Goldbach partitions: %d (at n=%d)" % (worst[0], worst[1]),
        "Counterexamples found: 0",
        "Range limit: this verifies Goldbach only up to %d, not for all even integers." % n,
    ])

def main():
    print("Unsolved equation program: Goldbach Conjecture")
    print("Edition: public")
    print("STATUS: OPEN")
    print("Statement: Every even integer n > 2 can be written as n = p + q with p and q prime.")
    payload = eng_goldbach()
    print(payload)
    print("What this does NOT prove: verifying Goldbach partitions up to 1000, which does not establish the conjecture for all even integers")
    print("Attempt summary: this public record carries no solving attempt; see the Signature mirror record.")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Twin Prime Conjecture JAH-USEQNT-000002 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record covers {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} The twin prime conjecture asserts that there are infinitely many pairs of primes that differ by 2, such as (3, 5), (5, 7), (11, 13), (17, 19), and (101, 103). It is the simplest question about patterns in the primes: the primes thin out as numbers grow, but do pairs of neighbouring primes keep appearing forever, or do they eventually stop? The prime number theorem says a number near x is prime with probability about 1/log x, so a heuristic count suggests about x/(log x)^2 twin pairs below x, growing without bound. No one has turned that heuristic into a demonstration, and no one has found a largest twin pair either.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: There are infinitely many primes p such that p + 2 is also prime. The twin prime conjecture asserts that there are infinitely many pairs of primes that differ by 2, such as (3, 5), (5, 7), (11, 13), (17, 19), and (101, 103). It is the simplest question about patterns in the primes: the primes thin out as numbers grow, but do pairs of neighbouring primes keep appearing forever, or do they eventually stop? The prime number theorem says a number near x is prime with probability about 1/log x, so a heuristic count suggests about x/(log x)^2 twin pairs below x, growing without bound. No one has turned that heuristic into a demonstration, and no one has found a largest twin pair either.

History

Euclid showed the primes are infinite more than two thousand years ago, but the question of twin primes was only posed in its modern form in the nineteenth century. Alphonse de Polignac conjectured in 1849 that every even gap between primes occurs infinitely often, which includes the twin case. In 1919 Viggo Brun proved that the sum of the reciprocals of the twin primes converges, showing twin primes are rarer than the primes themselves in a precise sense. The subject changed in 2013 when Yitang Zhang proved that some even gap below 70 million occurs infinitely often, and the Polymath project then reduced the bound to 246.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the primes are irregular in exactly the way the available methods cannot handle. Sieve methods cannot distinguish a prime from a product of two primes, which is the parity problem, and the circle method needs bounds on exponential sums that are far beyond current technology. Computation verifies the claim to enormous bounds, but a finite check is not a demonstration, and the history of number theory contains examples where a pattern held for a very long range and then failed. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a finite range and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

Zhang's 2013 theorem gives infinitely many prime pairs with gap at most 70,000,000, improved by the Polymath8 project to 246, and to 6 under the Elliott-Halberstam conjecture. Brun's theorem says the sum of reciprocals of twin primes converges to Brun's constant, about 1.90216. The largest known twin pair has hundreds of thousands of digits. The Hardy-Littlewood conjecture predicts the count of twin pairs below x is about 2 C_2 x/(log x)^2 with C_2 about 0.66016. What is missing is any argument that the gap 2 itself occurs infinitely often; all current methods produce bounded gaps but cannot force the smallest one.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQNTS-000002 presents an attempted approach to Twin Prime Conjecture, labelled there as an unverified attempt, and nothing in that record is a verified solution. The public record states the problem, its history, and what is known, and it does not claim a solution.

Math Proof

Let pi_2(x) count primes p <= x with p + 2 prime. The Hardy-Littlewood prediction is pi_2(x) ~ 2 C_2 x/(log x)^2, where C_2 = prod_{p>=3} (1 - 1/(p-1)^2). Sieve methods bound pi_2(x) from above by a constant multiple of x/(log x)^2, matching the order of magnitude, but lower bounds of the right order are out of reach. Zhang's argument uses the distribution of primes in arithmetic progressions: the Bombieri-Vinogradov theorem gives level of distribution 1/2, and the GPY sieve converts a level above 1/2 into bounded gaps. The twin case needs level 1, far beyond what is known. Formally the claim is: for every N there is a prime p > N with p + 2 prime.

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: There are infinitely many primes p such that p + 2 is also prime. The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Science Proof

A solution to Twin Prime Conjecture would reshape number theory. The primes are the atoms of arithmetic, and every statement about their distribution feeds into cryptography, where the security of widely used systems rests on the difficulty of factoring and on the behaviour of primes, into the theory of L-functions, where the zeros encode the primes, and into the rest of mathematics through the many theorems that are proved conditionally on open problems like this one. Even the partial results have paid off: the sieve methods developed for these problems are now used across number theory, and the computational methods have driven advances in distributed computing. The problem matters because it is a clean, precise question about the most basic objects in mathematics, and its answer would tell us how much of the apparent order in the primes is real.

Status

STATUS: OPEN

The problem Twin Prime Conjecture is open. It has been verified on finite ranges and studied by the methods described in this record, but no argument settles it in general. The status would change only on a peer-reviewed, accepted demonstration or a verified counterexample, and neither exists as of this record.

History

Euclid showed the primes are infinite more than two thousand years ago, but the question of twin primes was only posed in its modern form in the nineteenth century. Alphonse de Polignac conjectured in 1849 that every even gap between primes occurs infinitely often, which includes the twin case. In 1919 Viggo Brun proved that the sum of the reciprocals of the twin primes converges, showing twin primes are rarer than the primes themselves in a precise sense. The subject changed in 2013 when Yitang Zhang proved that some even gap below 70 million occurs infinitely often, and the Polymath project then reduced the bound to 246.

Problem

The problem, stated exactly, is: There are infinitely many primes p such that p + 2 is also prime. The twin prime conjecture asserts that there are infinitely many pairs of primes that differ by 2, such as (3, 5), (5, 7), (11, 13), (17, 19), and (101, 103). It is the simplest question about patterns in the primes: the primes thin out as numbers grow, but do pairs of neighbouring primes keep appearing forever, or do they eventually stop? The prime number theorem says a number near x is prime with probability about 1/log x, so a heuristic count suggests about x/(log x)^2 twin pairs below x, growing without bound. No one has turned that heuristic into a demonstration, and no one has found a largest twin pair either.

Method

The problem is approached with the classical tools of number theory: sieve methods that count integers with prescribed prime factors, the circle method that counts representations by an integral over exponential sums, and direct computation that verifies the claim up to a large bound. For Twin Prime Conjecture, the sieve gives upper bounds of the right order of magnitude, the circle method gives a conjectural asymptotic formula for the count of solutions, and computation checks the claim on a finite range. None of these methods closes the gap between what is known and what is claimed, because the parity problem blocks sieves from producing primes, and the circle method needs estimates on exponential sums that are not available. The demo in this record runs the computational part of the approach on a small range and reports exactly what it finds.

Verification path

A reader can check this record in three ways. First, follow the sources: each source is a real reference that states the problem and its status, and the two sources agree that the problem is open. Second, run the demo and the program: both are pure Python using only the standard library, both are deterministic, and both print their own SHA-256 digests so that two runs can be compared. Third, check the partial results against the literature: the bounds and counts quoted in this record are the published ones, and the record states where each number comes from. Independent verification of the open problem itself would require a new mathematical argument, peer-reviewed and accepted, and no such argument is claimed here.

Formal equation, in plain words

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: There are infinitely many primes p such that p + 2 is also prime. The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Proof sketch

The strongest established partial result is described in the record's account of what is known, and the sketch below follows it. Zhang's 2013 theorem gives infinitely many prime pairs with gap at most 70,000,000, improved by the Polymath8 project to 246, and to 6 under the Elliott-Halberstam conjecture. Brun's theorem says the sum of reciprocals of twin primes converges to Brun's constant, about 1.90216. The largest known twin pair has hundreds of thousands of digits. The Hardy-Littlewood conjecture predicts the count of twin pairs below x is about 2 C_2 x/(log x)^2 with C_2 about 0.66016. What is missing is any argument that the gap 2 itself occurs infinitely often; all current methods produce bounded gaps but cannot force the smallest one. The sketch shows the shape of the argument: a main term is identified, an error term is bounded, and the bound is too weak to reach the claim. The limits of the sketch are stated with it: the argument works on a finite range or under an unproved hypothesis, and it does not extend to the full claim. That is the honest state of the art for this problem.

Results reported

Zhang's 2013 theorem gives infinitely many prime pairs with gap at most 70,000,000, improved by the Polymath8 project to 246, and to 6 under the Elliott-Halberstam conjecture. Brun's theorem says the sum of reciprocals of twin primes converges to Brun's constant, about 1.90216. The largest known twin pair has hundreds of thousands of digits. The Hardy-Littlewood conjecture predicts the count of twin pairs below x is about 2 C_2 x/(log x)^2 with C_2 about 0.66016. What is missing is any argument that the gap 2 itself occurs infinitely often; all current methods produce bounded gaps but cannot force the smallest one.

Skeptical analysis

The problem resists solution because the primes are irregular in exactly the way the available methods cannot handle. Sieve methods cannot distinguish a prime from a product of two primes, which is the parity problem, and the circle method needs bounds on exponential sums that are far beyond current technology. Computation verifies the claim to enormous bounds, but a finite check is not a demonstration, and the history of number theory contains examples where a pattern held for a very long range and then failed. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a finite range and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_twin(n=10000):
    ps = primes_upto(n)
    s = set(ps)
    pairs = [(p, p + 2) for p in ps if p + 2 <= n and (p + 2) in s]
    return "\n".join([
        "Twin prime check: primes up to %d enumerated." % n,
        "Twin prime pairs found: %d" % len(pairs),
        "First 10 twin pairs: %s" % str(pairs[:10]),
        "Largest twin pair below %d: %s" % (n, str(pairs[-1])),
        "Range limit: this counts twin primes only up to %d; infinitude is not addressed." % n,
    ])

payload = eng_twin()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Twin Prime Conjecture")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: counting twin prime pairs up to 10000, which does not establish that infinitely many exist")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

Full working program

import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_twin(n=10000):
    ps = primes_upto(n)
    s = set(ps)
    pairs = [(p, p + 2) for p in ps if p + 2 <= n and (p + 2) in s]
    return "\n".join([
        "Twin prime check: primes up to %d enumerated." % n,
        "Twin prime pairs found: %d" % len(pairs),
        "First 10 twin pairs: %s" % str(pairs[:10]),
        "Largest twin pair below %d: %s" % (n, str(pairs[-1])),
        "Range limit: this counts twin primes only up to %d; infinitude is not addressed." % n,
    ])

def main():
    print("Unsolved equation program: Twin Prime Conjecture")
    print("Edition: public")
    print("STATUS: OPEN")
    print("Statement: There are infinitely many primes p such that p + 2 is also prime.")
    payload = eng_twin()
    print(payload)
    print("What this does NOT prove: counting twin prime pairs up to 10000, which does not establish that infinitely many exist")
    print("Attempt summary: this public record carries no solving attempt; see the Signature mirror record.")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Collatz Conjecture JAH-USEQNT-000003 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record covers {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} The Collatz conjecture, also called the 3n + 1 problem, starts with any positive integer and applies a simple rule: halve it when it is even, triple it and add one when it is odd, and repeat. The claim is that every starting value eventually reaches 1, after which the orbit cycles 1, 4, 2, 1. For example, starting at 27 the orbit climbs to 9232 before falling to 1 after 111 steps. The rule is so simple that a child can follow it, and the claim has been checked for starting values up to about 2^68, yet no argument covers all starting values, and no one knows whether some orbit might grow forever or fall into a different cycle.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: Starting from any positive integer n, repeatedly replace n by n/2 if n is even and by 3n + 1 if n is odd; the orbit always reaches 1. The Collatz conjecture, also called the 3n + 1 problem, starts with any positive integer and applies a simple rule: halve it when it is even, triple it and add one when it is odd, and repeat. The claim is that every starting value eventually reaches 1, after which the orbit cycles 1, 4, 2, 1. For example, starting at 27 the orbit climbs to 9232 before falling to 1 after 111 steps. The rule is so simple that a child can follow it, and the claim has been checked for starting values up to about 2^68, yet no argument covers all starting values, and no one knows whether some orbit might grow forever or fall into a different cycle.

History

Lothar Collatz proposed the problem in 1937 after studying iteration of arithmetic functions. It circulated by word of mouth for decades, reaching the United States in the 1950s and becoming famous as a problem that resisted every attack. In 1976 Richard Crandall noted that the heuristic behaviour is a random walk with a downward drift, which explains why orbits almost always fall. Terence Tao showed in 2019 that almost all orbits reach values far below their starting point, in the precise sense that the set of exceptions has density zero. The conjecture is often quoted as an example of a problem where computation gives overwhelming evidence and theory gives almost nothing.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the primes are irregular in exactly the way the available methods cannot handle. Sieve methods cannot distinguish a prime from a product of two primes, which is the parity problem, and the circle method needs bounds on exponential sums that are far beyond current technology. Computation verifies the claim to enormous bounds, but a finite check is not a demonstration, and the history of number theory contains examples where a pattern held for a very long range and then failed. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a finite range and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

The conjecture has been verified for all starting values up to about 2^68 by distributed computation. Tao's 2019 theorem shows that almost all Collatz orbits reach a value below any given function tending to infinity, so the set of possible counterexamples has density zero. The heuristic model treats the parity of each iterate as random, giving an expected contraction factor of (3/4)^(1/2) per two steps, which explains the observed behaviour. No non-trivial cycle other than 1, 4, 2 has been found, and Steiner showed in 1977 that there is no cycle with exactly one odd term; lower bounds on the length of any other cycle run into the millions of terms. A divergent orbit has never been observed.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQNTS-000003 presents an attempted approach to Collatz Conjecture, labelled there as an unverified attempt, and nothing in that record is a verified solution. The public record states the problem, its history, and what is known, and it does not claim a solution.

Math Proof

Define T(n) = n/2 for even n and (3n+1)/2 for odd n, the accelerated map. The conjecture is that iterating T from any positive integer reaches 1. The parity vector of an orbit determines its growth: each odd step multiplies roughly by 3/2 and each even step by 1/2, so an orbit with a fraction f of odd steps grows like (3^f / 2)^(steps). The 2-adic viewpoint extends T to the 2-adic integers, where the map is measure-preserving and ergodic, and Tao's argument uses the fact that the 2-adic distribution of orbits is nearly uniform. A cycle would satisfy an exponential Diophantine equation of the form (2^a - 3^b) dividing a fixed integer, and bounds on such equations come from lower bounds for linear forms in logarithms.

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: Starting from any positive integer n, repeatedly replace n by n/2 if n is even and by 3n + 1 if n is odd; the orbit always reaches 1. The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Science Proof

A solution to Collatz Conjecture would reshape number theory. The primes are the atoms of arithmetic, and every statement about their distribution feeds into cryptography, where the security of widely used systems rests on the difficulty of factoring and on the behaviour of primes, into the theory of L-functions, where the zeros encode the primes, and into the rest of mathematics through the many theorems that are proved conditionally on open problems like this one. Even the partial results have paid off: the sieve methods developed for these problems are now used across number theory, and the computational methods have driven advances in distributed computing. The problem matters because it is a clean, precise question about the most basic objects in mathematics, and its answer would tell us how much of the apparent order in the primes is real.

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_collatz(n=10000):
    def steps(x):
        c = 0
        while x != 1:
            x = x // 2 if x % 2 == 0 else 3 * x + 1
            c += 1
            if c > 100000:
                return -1
        return c
    mx = (0, None)
    total = 0
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        st = steps(i)
        assert st >= 0
        total += st
        if st > mx[0]:
            mx = (st, i)
    return "\n".join([
        "Collatz check: every starting value from 1 to %d tested." % n,
        "All reached 1: True",
        "Longest orbit: %d steps (starting at %d)" % (mx[0], mx[1]),
        "Total steps over all starts: %d" % total,
        "Range limit: this verifies Collatz only up to %d, not for all positive integers." % n,
    ])

payload = eng_collatz()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Collatz Conjecture")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: verifying that all starting values up to 10000 reach 1, which does not establish the claim for all positive integers")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

Full working program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_collatz(n=10000):
    def steps(x):
        c = 0
        while x != 1:
            x = x // 2 if x % 2 == 0 else 3 * x + 1
            c += 1
            if c > 100000:
                return -1
        return c
    mx = (0, None)
    total = 0
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        st = steps(i)
        assert st >= 0
        total += st
        if st > mx[0]:
            mx = (st, i)
    return "\n".join([
        "Collatz check: every starting value from 1 to %d tested." % n,
        "All reached 1: True",
        "Longest orbit: %d steps (starting at %d)" % (mx[0], mx[1]),
        "Total steps over all starts: %d" % total,
        "Range limit: this verifies Collatz only up to %d, not for all positive integers." % n,
    ])

def main():
    print("Unsolved equation program: Collatz Conjecture")
    print("Edition: public")
    print("STATUS: OPEN")
    print("Statement: Starting from any positive integer n, repeatedly replace n by n/2 if n is even and by 3n + 1 if n is odd; the orbit always reaches 1.")
    payload = eng_collatz()
    print(payload)
    print("What this does NOT prove: verifying that all starting values up to 10000 reach 1, which does not establish the claim for all positive integers")
    print("Attempt summary: this public record carries no solving attempt; see the Signature mirror record.")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Beal Conjecture JAH-USEQNT-000004 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record covers {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} The Beal conjecture says that the equation x^p + y^q = z^r, with all exponents greater than 2, has no solution in positive integers unless the three bases share a common prime factor. It generalises Fermat's Last Theorem, which is the special case p = q = r: Fermat says there is no solution at all with coprime bases, and Beal allows the exponents to differ while keeping the conclusion that a solution forces a common factor. Examples with a common factor are easy, such as 3^3 + 6^3 = 3^5, where 3 divides every base. The conjecture claims that no solution escapes this pattern, and a million-dollar prize is offered for its resolution.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: If x^p + y^q = z^r with x, y, z positive integers and p, q, r > 2, then x, y and z share a common prime factor. The Beal conjecture says that the equation x^p + y^q = z^r, with all exponents greater than 2, has no solution in positive integers unless the three bases share a common prime factor. It generalises Fermat's Last Theorem, which is the special case p = q = r: Fermat says there is no solution at all with coprime bases, and Beal allows the exponents to differ while keeping the conclusion that a solution forces a common factor. Examples with a common factor are easy, such as 3^3 + 6^3 = 3^5, where 3 divides every base. The conjecture claims that no solution escapes this pattern, and a million-dollar prize is offered for its resolution.

History

Andrew Beal, a Texas banker and amateur mathematician, formulated the conjecture in 1993 while studying generalisations of Fermat's Last Theorem, and he funded a prize that has grown to one million dollars for a demonstration or a counterexample. The conjecture is known to follow from the abc conjecture, and it implies Fermat's Last Theorem as a special case. Darmon and Granville showed in 1995 that for fixed exponents the equation has only finitely many solutions with coprime bases, using Faltings' theorem on curves of genus greater than one. Work on the generalised Fermat equation has settled many individual exponent triples, such as (2, 3, 7) and (2, 3, 11), by the modular method.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the primes are irregular in exactly the way the available methods cannot handle. Sieve methods cannot distinguish a prime from a product of two primes, which is the parity problem, and the circle method needs bounds on exponential sums that are far beyond current technology. Computation verifies the claim to enormous bounds, but a finite check is not a demonstration, and the history of number theory contains examples where a pattern held for a very long range and then failed. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a finite range and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

The conjecture is open in general. For fixed exponents (p, q, r) the Darmon-Granville theorem gives finiteness of coprime solutions, and the modular method of Frey, Serre, Ribet, Wiles and Darmon has resolved many specific triples, including all cases where one exponent is 2 and the others are small. Computational searches have found no counterexample with bases up to large bounds. The abc conjecture would imply Beal, since a coprime solution would give an abc triple of high quality. The million-dollar Beal Prize, administered by the American Mathematical Society, remains unclaimed. No solution with coprime bases is known for any exponent triple.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQNTS-000004 presents an attempted approach to Beal Conjecture, labelled there as an unverified attempt, and nothing in that record is a verified solution. The public record states the problem, its history, and what is known, and it does not claim a solution.

Math Proof

The equation x^p + y^q = z^r with coprime bases defines a generalised Fermat equation. The modular method attaches a Frey curve to a hypothetical solution and uses level lowering to derive a contradiction from the non-existence of certain modular forms; this works when the exponents are small enough for the relevant modular curves to have genus zero or one. The Darmon-Granville finiteness result views the equation as a curve of genus greater than one for fixed exponents and applies Faltings' theorem. In abc terms, a coprime solution with a + b = c and c a perfect r-th power would force rad(abc) to be small relative to c, contradicting the abc conjecture. Formally: for all x, y, z, p, q, r with p, q, r > 2 and x^p + y^q = z^r, gcd(x, y, z) > 1.

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: If x^p + y^q = z^r with x, y, z positive integers and p, q, r > 2, then x, y and z share a common prime factor. The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Science Proof

A solution to Beal Conjecture would reshape number theory. The primes are the atoms of arithmetic, and every statement about their distribution feeds into cryptography, where the security of widely used systems rests on the difficulty of factoring and on the behaviour of primes, into the theory of L-functions, where the zeros encode the primes, and into the rest of mathematics through the many theorems that are proved conditionally on open problems like this one. Even the partial results have paid off: the sieve methods developed for these problems are now used across number theory, and the computational methods have driven advances in distributed computing. The problem matters because it is a clean, precise question about the most basic objects in mathematics, and its answer would tell us how much of the apparent order in the primes is real.

Program

The full program prints the problem statement, the status line, and the same verified computation as the demo, followed by the attempt summary. It uses only the Python standard library and runs deterministically.

Status

STATUS: OPEN

The problem Beal Conjecture is open. It has been verified on finite ranges and studied by the methods described in this record, but no argument settles it in general. The status would change only on a peer-reviewed, accepted demonstration or a verified counterexample, and neither exists as of this record.

History

Andrew Beal, a Texas banker and amateur mathematician, formulated the conjecture in 1993 while studying generalisations of Fermat's Last Theorem, and he funded a prize that has grown to one million dollars for a demonstration or a counterexample. The conjecture is known to follow from the abc conjecture, and it implies Fermat's Last Theorem as a special case. Problem

The problem, stated exactly, is: If x^p + y^q = z^r with x, y, z positive integers and p, q, r > 2, then x, y and z share a common prime factor. The Beal conjecture says that the equation x^p + y^q = z^r, with all exponents greater than 2, has no solution in positive integers unless the three bases share a common prime factor. It generalises Fermat's Last Theorem, which is the special case p = q = r: Fermat says there is no solution at all with coprime bases, and Beal allows the exponents to differ while keeping the conclusion that a solution forces a common factor. Examples with a common factor are easy, such as 3^3 + 6^3 = 3^5, where 3 divides every base. The conjecture claims that no solution escapes this pattern, and a million-dollar prize is offered for its resolution.

Method

The problem is approached with the classical tools of number theory: sieve methods that count integers with prescribed prime factors, the circle method that counts representations by an integral over exponential sums, and direct computation that verifies the claim up to a large bound. For Beal Conjecture, the sieve gives upper bounds of the right order of magnitude, the circle method gives a conjectural asymptotic formula for the count of solutions, and computation checks the claim on a finite range. None of these methods closes the gap between what is known and what is claimed, because the parity problem blocks sieves from producing primes, and the circle method needs estimates on exponential sums that are not available. The demo in this record runs the computational part of the approach on a small range and reports exactly what it finds.

Verification path

Second, run the demo and the program: both are pure Python using only the standard library, both are deterministic, and both print their own SHA-256 digests so that two runs can be compared. Third, check the partial results against the literature: the bounds and counts quoted in this record are the published ones, and the record states where each number comes from. Independent verification of the open problem itself would require a new mathematical argument, peer-reviewed and accepted, and no such argument is claimed here.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_beal(bound=20):
    vals = {}
    for x in range(2, bound + 1):
        for p in (3, 4, 5):
            vals.setdefault(x ** p, []).append((x, p))
    hits = []
    for x in range(2, bound + 1):
        for y in range(2, bound + 1):
            for p in (3, 4, 5):
                for q in (3, 4, 5):
                    s = x ** p + y ** q
                    for z in range(2, bound + 1):
                        for r in (3, 4, 5):
                            if z ** r == s and math.gcd(math.gcd(x, y), z) == 1:
                                hits.append((x, p, y, q, z, r))
    return "\n".join([
        "Beal check: x^p + y^q = z^r with coprime bases, 2 <= x,y,z <= %d, exponents 3..5." % bound,
        "Counterexamples found: %s" % str(hits),
        "Count: %d" % len(hits),
        "Range limit: a small bounded search; Beal concerns all exponents and bases.",
    ])

payload = eng_beal()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Beal Conjecture")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: searching for coprime solutions with bases up to 20 and exponents 3 to 5, which does not establish the conjecture in general")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

        for y in range(2, bound + 1):
            for p in (3, 4, 5):
                for q in (3, 4, 5):
                    s = x ** p + y ** q
                    for z in range(2, bound + 1):
                        for r in (3, 4, 5):
                            if z ** r == s and math.gcd(math.gcd(x, y), z) == 1:
                                hits.append((x, p, y, q, z, r))
    return "\n".join([
        "Beal check: x^p + y^q = z^r with coprime bases, 2 <= x,y,z <= %d, exponents 3..5." % bound,
        "Counterexamples found: %s" % str(hits),
        "Count: %d" % len(hits),
        "Range limit: a small bounded search; Beal concerns all exponents and bases.",
    ])

def main():
    print("Unsolved equation program: Beal Conjecture")
    print("Edition: public")
    print("STATUS: OPEN")
    print("Statement: If x^p + y^q = z^r with x, y, z positive integers and p, q, r > 2, then x, y and z share a common prime factor.")
    payload = eng_beal()
    print(payload)
    print("What this does NOT prove: searching for coprime solutions with bases up to 20 and exponents 3 to 5, which does not establish the conjecture in general")
    print("Attempt summary: this public record carries no solving attempt; see the Signature mirror record.")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

abc Conjecture JAH-USEQNT-000005 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record covers {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} The abc conjecture concerns the equation a + b = c with a, b, c coprime positive integers. Let rad(n) be the product of the distinct prime factors of n, so rad(72) = 2 x 3 = 6. The conjecture says that c is usually not much larger than rad(abc): for any epsilon > 0, only finitely many triples have c > rad(abc)^(1 + epsilon). In other words, a sum of two numbers that are both built from small primes cannot itself be a large number built from small primes, except finitely many times. The quality q = log c / log rad(abc) measures how extreme a triple is, and the conjecture says the quality cannot exceed 1 except finitely often.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: For every epsilon > 0 there are only finitely many coprime triples a + b = c with c > rad(abc)^(1 + epsilon), where rad(n) is the product of the distinct primes dividing n. The abc conjecture concerns the equation a + b = c with a, b, c coprime positive integers. Let rad(n) be the product of the distinct prime factors of n, so rad(72) = 2 x 3 = 6. The conjecture says that c is usually not much larger than rad(abc): for any epsilon > 0, only finitely many triples have c > rad(abc)^(1 + epsilon). In other words, a sum of two numbers that are both built from small primes cannot itself be a large number built from small primes, except finitely many times. The quality q = log c / log rad(abc) measures how extreme a triple is, and the conjecture says the quality cannot exceed 1 except finitely often.

History

Joseph Oesterle and David Masser formulated the conjecture in 1985, motivated by the analogy between integers and polynomials, where the corresponding statement is a theorem of Mason and Stothers. The conjecture quickly became central because it implies a host of famous results: Fermat's Last Theorem for large exponents, the Mordell conjecture, Roth's theorem, and the Beal conjecture. In 2012 Shinichi Mochizuki released a claimed demonstration using inter-universal Teichmüller theory, but the mathematical community has not accepted the argument, and the claim remains disputed and unverified. The conjecture is regarded as one of the most important open problems in number theory.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the primes are irregular in exactly the way the available methods cannot handle. Sieve methods cannot distinguish a prime from a product of two primes, which is the parity problem, and the circle method needs bounds on exponential sums that are far beyond current technology. Computation verifies the claim to enormous bounds, but a finite check is not a demonstration, and the history of number theory contains examples where a pattern held for a very long range and then failed. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a finite range and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

The best unconditional results are far from the conjecture: Stewart and Tijdeman, and later improvements, give bounds of the form c < exp(C rad(abc)^(1/3) (log rad(abc))^3) and similar. The highest-quality known triple is 2 + 3^10 x 109 = 23^5, with quality about 1.6299, found by Eric Reyssat. The conjecture would imply the finiteness of solutions to many exponential Diophantine equations and a uniform bound on the number of solutions to Mordell curves. Mochizuki's claimed demonstration has not been accepted; the 2018 discussion between Mochizuki and Scholze-Stix ended without agreement, and no verification has been published in a form the community accepts.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQNTS-000005 presents an attempted approach to abc Conjecture, labelled there as an unverified attempt, and nothing in that record is a verified solution. The public record states the problem, its history, and what is known, and it does not claim a solution.

Math Proof

For a triple a + b = c with gcd(a, b) = 1, define the radical rad(abc) and the quality q(a, b, c) = log c / log rad(abc). The conjecture is that lim sup of q over all triples is 1. The polynomial analogue, the Mason-Stothers theorem, says that for coprime polynomials a + b = c the degree of c is at most the number of distinct roots of abc minus 1, and its demonstration uses derivatives, which have no integer analogue. The abc conjecture is equivalent to a statement about the Szpiro conjecture for elliptic curves: the discriminant of an elliptic curve is bounded by a power of its conductor. Formally: for every epsilon > 0 the set { (a,b,c) : gcd(a,b)=1, a+b=c, c > rad(abc)^(1+epsilon) } is finite.

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: For every epsilon > 0 there are only finitely many coprime triples a + b = c with c > rad(abc)^(1 + epsilon), where rad(n) is the product of the distinct primes dividing n. The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Science Proof

A solution to abc Conjecture would reshape number theory. The primes are the atoms of arithmetic, and every statement about their distribution feeds into cryptography, where the security of widely used systems rests on the difficulty of factoring and on the behaviour of primes, into the theory of L-functions, where the zeros encode the primes, and into the rest of mathematics through the many theorems that are proved conditionally on open problems like this one. Even the partial results have paid off: the sieve methods developed for these problems are now used across number theory, and the computational methods have driven advances in distributed computing. The problem matters because it is a clean, precise question about the most basic objects in mathematics, and its answer would tell us how much of the apparent order in the primes is real.

Status

STATUS: OPEN

The problem abc Conjecture is open. It has been verified on finite ranges and studied by the methods described in this record, but no argument settles it in general. The status would change only on a peer-reviewed, accepted demonstration or a verified counterexample, and neither exists as of this record.

History

Joseph Oesterle and David Masser formulated the conjecture in 1985, motivated by the analogy between integers and polynomials, where the corresponding statement is a theorem of Mason and Stothers. The conjecture quickly became central because it implies a host of famous results: Fermat's Last Theorem for large exponents, the Mordell conjecture, Roth's theorem, and the Beal conjecture. In 2012 Shinichi Mochizuki released a claimed demonstration using inter-universal Teichmüller theory, but the mathematical community has not accepted the argument, and the claim remains disputed and unverified. The conjecture is regarded as one of the most important open problems in number theory.

Problem

The problem, stated exactly, is: For every epsilon > 0 there are only finitely many coprime triples a + b = c with c > rad(abc)^(1 + epsilon), where rad(n) is the product of the distinct primes dividing n. The abc conjecture concerns the equation a + b = c with a, b, c coprime positive integers. Let rad(n) be the product of the distinct prime factors of n, so rad(72) = 2 x 3 = 6. The conjecture says that c is usually not much larger than rad(abc): for any epsilon > 0, only finitely many triples have c > rad(abc)^(1 + epsilon). In other words, a sum of two numbers that are both built from small primes cannot itself be a large number built from small primes, except finitely many times. The quality q = log c / log rad(abc) measures how extreme a triple is, and the conjecture says the quality cannot exceed 1 except finitely often.

Method

The problem is approached with the classical tools of number theory: sieve methods that count integers with prescribed prime factors, the circle method that counts representations by an integral over exponential sums, and direct computation that verifies the claim up to a large bound. For abc Conjecture, the sieve gives upper bounds of the right order of magnitude, the circle method gives a conjectural asymptotic formula for the count of solutions, and computation checks the claim on a finite range. None of these methods closes the gap between what is known and what is claimed, because the parity problem blocks sieves from producing primes, and the circle method needs estimates on exponential sums that are not available. The demo in this record runs the computational part of the approach on a small range and reports exactly what it finds.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_abc(cmax=200):
    best = (0.0, None)
    below = 0
    total = 0
    for c in range(3, cmax + 1):
        for a in range(1, c):
            b = c - a
            if b <= a:
                continue
            if math.gcd(math.gcd(a, b), c) != 1:
                continue
            total += 1
            r = rad(a * b * c)
            if r < c:
                below += 1
            q = math.log(c) / math.log(r) if r > 1 else 0.0
            if q > best[0]:
                best = (q, (a, b, c, r))
    return "\n".join([
        "abc check: coprime triples a + b = c with c <= %d." % cmax,
        "Triples tested: %d" % total,
        "Triples with rad(abc) < c: %d" % below,
        "Highest quality log(c)/log(rad): %.4f at (a,b,c,rad) = %s" % (best[0], str(best[1])),
        "Range limit: c <= %d only; the abc conjecture bounds quality for all triples." % cmax,
    ])

payload = eng_abc()
print("Unsolved equation demo: abc Conjecture")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: computing the quality of coprime triples with c up to 200, which does not establish the finiteness claim")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")
