Gilbreath Conjecture JAH-USEQNT-000011 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record covers {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} The Gilbreath conjecture starts with the sequence of prime numbers and forms a new sequence from the absolute differences of consecutive terms: from 2, 3, 5, 7, 11, 13 the next row is 1, 2, 2, 4, 2, and the row after that is 1, 0, 2, 2. Repeating this process gives a triangular array, and the conjecture asserts that the first entry of every row is 1. The claim has been verified for an enormous number of rows, up to primes around 10^13, and the pattern is striking: the leading 1 seems indestructible. Yet the primes are irregular, the differences can be large, and no argument explains why the first column should always be 1.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: Start with the primes 2, 3, 5, 7, 11, ... and repeatedly replace the sequence by the absolute differences of consecutive terms. Is the first term of every row 1? The Gilbreath conjecture starts with the sequence of prime numbers and forms a new sequence from the absolute differences of consecutive terms: from 2, 3, 5, 7, 11, 13 the next row is 1, 2, 2, 4, 2, and the row after that is 1, 0, 2, 2. Repeating this process gives a triangular array, and the conjecture asserts that the first entry of every row is 1. The claim has been verified for an enormous number of rows, up to primes around 10^13, and the pattern is striking: the leading 1 seems indestructible. Yet the primes are irregular, the differences can be large, and no argument explains why the first column should always be 1.

History

Norman Gilbreath, a mathematician at the University of California, noticed the pattern in 1958 while experimenting with prime differences, and the conjecture circulated as a curiosity. It is related to the older question of prime gaps: the first differences are the prime gaps, and the iterated differences mix those gaps together. In 1993 Andrew Odlyzko verified the conjecture up to primes near 10^13, covering about 3.4 x 10^11 rows, by a clever argument that only needs the primes in intervals where the gaps are small. The conjecture has become a standard example of a simply stated problem about primes that computation supports overwhelmingly and theory cannot touch.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the primes are irregular in exactly the way the available methods cannot handle. Sieve methods cannot distinguish a prime from a product of two primes, which is the parity problem, and the circle method needs bounds on exponential sums that are far beyond current technology. Computation verifies the claim to enormous bounds, but a finite check is not a demonstration, and the history of number theory contains examples where a pattern held for a very long range and then failed. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a finite range and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

The conjecture has been verified for the first 3.4 x 10^11 rows by Odlyzko, using primes up to about 10^13. The verification works because the iterated differences only depend on local prime gaps, and prime gaps up to 10^13 are known to be small enough for the argument to go through. No counterexample is known, and the first column has been 1 in every row ever computed. There is no theorem that the first entry is always 1, and there is no heuristic model that predicts the behaviour precisely, although the small size of prime gaps relative to the primes makes the pattern plausible. The problem is open in the strong sense that no general approach exists.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQNTS-000011 presents an attempted approach to Gilbreath Conjecture, labelled there as an unverified attempt, and nothing in that record is a verified solution. The public record states the problem, its history, and what is known, and it does not claim a solution.

Math Proof

Let p_1, p_2, ... be the primes and define d_0(n) = p_n and d_{k+1}(n) = |d_k(n+1) - d_k(n)|. The conjecture is d_k(1) = 1 for all k >= 1. The first differences d_1(n) are the prime gaps g_n = p_{n+1} - p_n, which are even for n >= 2. The iterated differences are integer linear combinations of the prime gaps with binomial coefficients, and the first column is a signed sum of the first few gaps with binomial weights. Odlyzko's verification uses the fact that if the prime gaps in a window are all at most some bound, then the iterated differences in that window stay controlled, and the first entry is forced to be 1. A general demonstration would need control of prime gaps far beyond current bounds, since gaps of size about (log p)^2 are expected and the argument needs gaps below a fixed multiple of the row index.

The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Science Proof

A solution to Gilbreath Conjecture would reshape number theory. The primes are the atoms of arithmetic, and every statement about their distribution feeds into cryptography, where the security of widely used systems rests on the difficulty of factoring and on the behaviour of primes, into the theory of L-functions, where the zeros encode the primes, and into the rest of mathematics through the many theorems that are proved conditionally on open problems like this one. Proof sketch

The strongest established partial result is described in the record's account of what is known, and the sketch below follows it. The conjecture has been verified for the first 3.4 x 10^11 rows by Odlyzko, using primes up to about 10^13. The verification works because the iterated differences only depend on local prime gaps, and prime gaps up to 10^13 are known to be small enough for the argument to go through. No counterexample is known, and the first column has been 1 in every row ever computed. There is no theorem that the first entry is always 1, and there is no heuristic model that predicts the behaviour precisely, although the small size of prime gaps relative to the primes makes the pattern plausible. The problem is open in the strong sense that no general approach exists. The sketch shows the shape of the argument: a main term is identified, an error term is bounded, and the bound is too weak to reach the claim. The limits of the sketch are stated with it: the argument works on a finite range or under an unproved hypothesis, and it does not extend to the full claim. That is the honest state of the art for this problem.

Skeptical analysis

The problem resists solution because the primes are irregular in exactly the way the available methods cannot handle. Sieve methods cannot distinguish a prime from a product of two primes, which is the parity problem, and the circle method needs bounds on exponential sums that are far beyond current technology. Computation verifies the claim to enormous bounds, but a finite check is not a demonstration, and the history of number theory contains examples where a pattern held for a very long range and then failed. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a finite range and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_gilbreath(rows=24):
    ps = primes_upto(1000)
    seq = ps[:25]
    rows = min(rows, len(seq) - 1)
    firsts = []
    cur = seq[:]
    for _ in range(rows):
        firsts.append(cur[0])
        cur = [abs(cur[i + 1] - cur[i]) for i in range(len(cur) - 1)]
    return "\n".join([
        "Gilbreath check: iterated absolute differences of the first 25 primes, %d rows." % rows,
        "First entry of each row: %s" % str(firsts[:20]),
        "All first entries equal to 1: %s" % all(f == 1 for f in firsts),
        "Range limit: 25 starting primes and %d rows; the conjecture is about all rows." % rows,
    ])

payload = eng_gilbreath()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Gilbreath Conjecture")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: iterating prime differences for 24 rows from the first 25 primes, which does not establish the claim for all rows")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_gilbreath(rows=24):
    ps = primes_upto(1000)
    seq = ps[:25]
    rows = min(rows, len(seq) - 1)
    firsts = []
    cur = seq[:]
    for _ in range(rows):
        firsts.append(cur[0])
        cur = [abs(cur[i + 1] - cur[i]) for i in range(len(cur) - 1)]
    return "\n".join([
        "Gilbreath check: iterated absolute differences of the first 25 primes, %d rows." % rows,
        "First entry of each row: %s" % str(firsts[:20]),
        "All first entries equal to 1: %s" % all(f == 1 for f in firsts),
        "Range limit: 25 starting primes and %d rows; the conjecture is about all rows." % rows,
    ])

def main():
    print("Unsolved equation program: Gilbreath Conjecture")
    print("Edition: public")
    print("STATUS: OPEN")
    print("Statement: Start with the primes 2, 3, 5, 7, 11, ... and repeatedly replace the sequence by the absolute differences of consecutive terms. Is the first term of every row 1?")
    payload = eng_gilbreath()
    print(payload)
    print("What this does NOT prove: iterating prime differences for 24 rows from the first 25 primes, which does not establish the claim for all rows")
    print("Attempt summary: this public record carries no solving attempt; see the Signature mirror record.")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Andrica Conjecture JAH-USEQNT-000012 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record covers {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} The Andrica conjecture asserts that the gap between the square roots of consecutive primes is always less than 1: sqrt(p_{n+1}) - sqrt(p_n) < 1 for every n. Since sqrt(p_{n+1}) - sqrt(p_n) = (p_{n+1} - p_n)/(sqrt(p_{n+1}) + sqrt(p_n)), the claim is equivalent to saying the prime gap g_n = p_{n+1} - p_n is always less than sqrt(p_{n+1}) + sqrt(p_n), which is about 2 sqrt(p_n). Prime gaps are expected to be about (log p)^2, far smaller than 2 sqrt(p), so the conjecture looks very safe. It has been verified for primes up to 10^18 and beyond, and it would follow from the Riemann hypothesis. But unconditionally, the best known prime gap bound is about p^0.525, which is larger than sqrt(p), so the conjecture remains open.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: For consecutive primes p_n and p_{n+1}, is sqrt(p_{n+1}) - sqrt(p_n) < 1 always? The Andrica conjecture asserts that the gap between the square roots of consecutive primes is always less than 1: sqrt(p_{n+1}) - sqrt(p_n) < 1 for every n. Since sqrt(p_{n+1}) - sqrt(p_n) = (p_{n+1} - p_n)/(sqrt(p_{n+1}) + sqrt(p_n)), the claim is equivalent to saying the prime gap g_n = p_{n+1} - p_n is always less than sqrt(p_{n+1}) + sqrt(p_n), which is about 2 sqrt(p_n). Prime gaps are expected to be about (log p)^2, far smaller than 2 sqrt(p), so the conjecture looks very safe. It has been verified for primes up to 10^18 and beyond, and it would follow from the Riemann hypothesis. But unconditionally, the best known prime gap bound is about p^0.525, which is larger than sqrt(p), so the conjecture remains open.

History

Dorin Andrica, a Romanian mathematician, stated the conjecture in 1985, motivated by the pattern of prime gaps. It is one of a family of conjectures about prime gaps that includes Legendre's and Oppermann's conjectures, all of which ask for primes in intervals of length about sqrt(x). The conjecture was verified computationally as prime tables grew, and Imran Ghory extended the verification to large ranges. In 2015, work on prime gaps showed the conjecture holds up to very large bounds, and it is known to follow from the Riemann hypothesis, which gives prime gaps of size O(sqrt(p) log p). The unconditional gap between the best known bound and the conjectured bound is the whole difficulty.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the primes are irregular in exactly the way the available methods cannot handle. Sieve methods cannot distinguish a prime from a product of two primes, which is the parity problem, and the circle method needs bounds on exponential sums that are far beyond current technology. Computation verifies the claim to enormous bounds, but a finite check is not a demonstration, and the history of number theory contains examples where a pattern held for a very long range and then failed. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a finite range and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

The conjecture has been verified for all primes up to at least 10^18. It follows from the Riemann hypothesis, since the hypothesis implies prime gaps below C sqrt(p) log p, and the constant can be made small enough. Unconditionally, the best prime gap result is the Baker-Harman-Pintz bound of O(p^0.525), which is weaker than what Andrica needs. The largest known value of sqrt(p_{n+1}) - sqrt(p_n) is about 0.67096, attained near p = 1,000,000,000,000,000,000, well below 1. The conjecture is closely related to Legendre's conjecture: if there is always a prime between n^2 and (n+1)^2, then Andrica follows, but the converse is not known.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQNTS-000012 presents an attempted approach to Andrica Conjecture, labelled there as an unverified attempt, and nothing in that record is a verified solution. The public record states the problem, its history, and what is known, and it does not claim a solution.

Math Proof

Write g_n = p_{n+1} - p_n. The Andrica inequality sqrt(p_{n+1}) - sqrt(p_n) < 1 is equivalent to g_n < sqrt(p_{n+1}) + sqrt(p_n) < 2 sqrt(p_{n+1}) + 1. The prime number theorem gives the average gap near p as log p, and Cramér's model predicts the maximal gap near p is about (log p)^2, so the expected margin is enormous. The Riemann hypothesis implies g_n = O(sqrt(p_n) log p_n) by the standard explicit formula argument, which is enough to give Andrica for large p. Unconditional methods use zero-density estimates for the zeta function to bound gaps, and the current exponent 0.525 exceeds the 0.5 that Andrica needs. Formally: for all n, sqrt(p_{n+1}) - sqrt(p_n) < 1.

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: For consecutive primes p_n and p_{n+1}, is sqrt(p_{n+1}) - sqrt(p_n) < 1 always? The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Science Proof

A solution to Andrica Conjecture would reshape number theory. The primes are the atoms of arithmetic, and every statement about their distribution feeds into cryptography, where the security of widely used systems rests on the difficulty of factoring and on the behaviour of primes, into the theory of L-functions, where the zeros encode the primes, and into the rest of mathematics through the many theorems that are proved conditionally on open problems like this one. Even the partial results have paid off: the sieve methods developed for these problems are now used across number theory, and the computational methods have driven advances in distributed computing. The problem matters because it is a clean, precise question about the most basic objects in mathematics, and its answer would tell us how much of the apparent order in the primes is real.

Always below 1: True

Status

STATUS: OPEN

The problem Andrica Conjecture is open. It has been verified on finite ranges and studied by the methods described in this record, but no argument settles it in general. The status would change only on a peer-reviewed, accepted demonstration or a verified counterexample, and neither exists as of this record.

History

Dorin Andrica, a Romanian mathematician, stated the conjecture in 1985, motivated by the pattern of prime gaps. It is one of a family of conjectures about prime gaps that includes Legendre's and Oppermann's conjectures, all of which ask for primes in intervals of length about sqrt(x). The conjecture was verified computationally as prime tables grew, and Imran Ghory extended the verification to large ranges. In 2015, work on prime gaps showed the conjecture holds up to very large bounds, and it is known to follow from the Riemann hypothesis, which gives prime gaps of size O(sqrt(p) log p). The unconditional gap between the best known bound and the conjectured bound is the whole difficulty.

Problem

The problem, stated exactly, is: For consecutive primes p_n and p_{n+1}, is sqrt(p_{n+1}) - sqrt(p_n) < 1 always? The Andrica conjecture asserts that the gap between the square roots of consecutive primes is always less than 1: sqrt(p_{n+1}) - sqrt(p_n) < 1 for every n. Since sqrt(p_{n+1}) - sqrt(p_n) = (p_{n+1} - p_n)/(sqrt(p_{n+1}) + sqrt(p_n)), the claim is equivalent to saying the prime gap g_n = p_{n+1} - p_n is always less than sqrt(p_{n+1}) + sqrt(p_n), which is about 2 sqrt(p_n). Prime gaps are expected to be about (log p)^2, far smaller than 2 sqrt(p), so the conjecture looks very safe. It has been verified for primes up to 10^18 and beyond, and it would follow from the Riemann hypothesis. But unconditionally, the best known prime gap bound is about p^0.525, which is larger than sqrt(p), so the conjecture remains open.

Method

The problem is approached with the classical tools of number theory: sieve methods that count integers with prescribed prime factors, the circle method that counts representations by an integral over exponential sums, and direct computation that verifies the claim up to a large bound. For Andrica Conjecture, the sieve gives upper bounds of the right order of magnitude, the circle method gives a conjectural asymptotic formula for the count of solutions, and computation checks the claim on a finite range. None of these methods closes the gap between what is known and what is claimed, because the parity problem blocks sieves from producing primes, and the circle method needs estimates on exponential sums that are not available. The demo in this record runs the computational part of the approach on a small range and reports exactly what it finds.

Verification path

Second, run the demo and the program: both are pure Python using only the standard library, both are deterministic, and both print their own SHA-256 digests so that two runs can be compared. Third, check the partial results against the literature: the bounds and counts quoted in this record are the published ones, and the record states where each number comes from. Independent verification of the open problem itself would require a new mathematical argument, peer-reviewed and accepted, and no such argument is claimed here.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_andrica(n=100000):
    ps = primes_upto(n)
    mx = 0.0
    arg = None
    for a, b in zip(ps, ps[1:]):
        d = math.sqrt(b) - math.sqrt(a)
        if d > mx:
            mx, arg = d, (a, b)
    return "\n".join([
        "Andrica check: sqrt(p_{k+1}) - sqrt(p_k) for primes up to %d." % n,
        "Largest gap in square roots: %.6f (between %d and %d)" % (mx, arg[0], arg[1]),
        "Always below 1: %s" % (mx < 1),
        "Range limit: primes only up to %d; the conjecture is about all primes." % n,
    ])

payload = eng_andrica()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Andrica Conjecture")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: checking the square-root gap inequality for primes up to 100000, which does not establish it for all primes")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

Unsolved equation demo: Andrica Conjecture
Andrica check: sqrt(p_{k+1}) - sqrt(p_k) for primes up to 100000.
Largest gap in square roots: 0.670873 (between 7 and 11)
Always below 1: True
Range limit: primes only up to 100000; the conjecture is about all primes.
What this does NOT prove: checking the square-root gap inequality for primes up to 100000, which does not establish it for all primes
Run 1 SHA-256: b7d6f9882862fe9634640680154ede862bc42e6c0525f021e89811de91d94776
Run 2 SHA-256: b7d6f9882862fe9634640680154ede862bc42e6c0525f021e89811de91d94776
Deterministic: MATCH

Full working program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_andrica(n=100000):
    ps = primes_upto(n)
    mx = 0.0
    arg = None
    for a, b in zip(ps, ps[1:]):
        d = math.sqrt(b) - math.sqrt(a)
        if d > mx:
            mx, arg = d, (a, b)
    return "\n".join([
        "Andrica check: sqrt(p_{k+1}) - sqrt(p_k) for primes up to %d." % n,
        "Largest gap in square roots: %.6f (between %d and %d)" % (mx, arg[0], arg[1]),
        "Always below 1: %s" % (mx < 1),
        "Range limit: primes only up to %d; the conjecture is about all primes." % n,
    ])

def main():
    print("Unsolved equation program: Andrica Conjecture")
    print("Edition: public")
    print("STATUS: OPEN")
    print("Statement: For consecutive primes p_n and p_{n+1}, is sqrt(p_{n+1}) - sqrt(p_n) < 1 always?")
    payload = eng_andrica()
    print(payload)
    print("What this does NOT prove: checking the square-root gap inequality for primes up to 100000, which does not establish it for all primes")
    print("Attempt summary: this public record carries no solving attempt; see the Signature mirror record.")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Oppermann Conjecture JAH-USEQNT-000013 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record covers {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} The Oppermann conjecture asserts that for every positive integer n there is a prime between n^2 and n(n+1), and also a prime between n(n+1) and (n+1)^2. It splits the interval between consecutive squares into two halves and demands a prime in each half, which is stronger than Legendre's conjecture, which only asks for one prime in the whole interval. For example, between 25 and 30 there is 29, and between 30 and 36 there is 31. The intervals have length n, and the average prime gap near n^2 is about 2 log n, so the claim is very plausible. It has been verified to large bounds, but no argument guarantees a prime in every such interval.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: Is there always a prime between n^2 and n(n+1), and another between n(n+1) and (n+1)^2, for every positive integer n? The Oppermann conjecture asserts that for every positive integer n there is a prime between n^2 and n(n+1), and also a prime between n(n+1) and (n+1)^2. It splits the interval between consecutive squares into two halves and demands a prime in each half, which is stronger than Legendre's conjecture, which only asks for one prime in the whole interval. For example, between 25 and 30 there is 29, and between 30 and 36 there is 31. The intervals have length n, and the average prime gap near n^2 is about 2 log n, so the claim is very plausible. It has been verified to large bounds, but no argument guarantees a prime in every such interval.

History

Ludvig Oppermann, a Danish mathematician, stated the conjecture in 1882 in a paper on the distribution of primes. It is one of the classical conjectures about primes in short intervals, alongside Legendre's conjecture of 1798. The two conjectures are related: Oppermann implies Legendre, since a prime in either half is a prime in the whole interval. In the twentieth century, results on primes in short intervals improved the unconditional exponent from 1 down to 0.525, but Oppermann needs intervals of length n, which is about sqrt(x), corresponding to exponent 0.5. The conjecture remains open, and it is known to follow from the Riemann hypothesis with room to spare.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the primes are irregular in exactly the way the available methods cannot handle. Sieve methods cannot distinguish a prime from a product of two primes, which is the parity problem, and the circle method needs bounds on exponential sums that are far beyond current technology. Computation verifies the claim to enormous bounds, but a finite check is not a demonstration, and the history of number theory contains examples where a pattern held for a very long range and then failed. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a finite range and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

The conjecture has been verified computationally for n up to very large bounds, at least 10^11 and beyond in the literature. The best unconditional theorem on primes in short intervals gives a prime between x and x + x^0.525, which does not reach the interval length sqrt(x) that Oppermann needs. Under the Riemann hypothesis, there is a prime between x and x + O(sqrt(x) log x), which implies Oppermann for large n. The conjecture is strictly stronger than Legendre's, and both are implied by Cramér's model of prime gaps, which predicts maximal gaps of order (log x)^2. No counterexample is known.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQNTS-000013 presents an attempted approach to Oppermann Conjecture, labelled there as an unverified attempt, and nothing in that record is a verified solution. The public record states the problem, its history, and what is known, and it does not claim a solution.

Math Proof

The conjecture is the pair of statements pi(n(n+1)) - pi(n^2) >= 1 and pi((n+1)^2) - pi(n(n+1)) >= 1 for all n >= 1. The prime number theorem predicts each interval contains about n/(2 log n) primes, tending to infinity. The difficulty is the same as for Legendre: unconditional prime gap bounds are O(x^0.525), while the intervals have length about x^0.5. The Riemann hypothesis gives gaps O(sqrt(x) log x), which implies Oppermann. Formally the claim is a universal statement over n about prime counts in intervals of length n around n^2, and it is one of the cleanest tests of our ignorance about the fine distribution of primes.

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: Is there always a prime between n^2 and n(n+1), and another between n(n+1) and (n+1)^2, for every positive integer n? The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Science Proof

A solution to Oppermann Conjecture would reshape number theory. The primes are the atoms of arithmetic, and every statement about their distribution feeds into cryptography, where the security of widely used systems rests on the difficulty of factoring and on the behaviour of primes, into the theory of L-functions, where the zeros encode the primes, and into the rest of mathematics through the many theorems that are proved conditionally on open problems like this one. Even the partial results have paid off: the sieve methods developed for these problems are now used across number theory, and the computational methods have driven advances in distributed computing. The problem matters because it is a clean, precise question about the most basic objects in mathematics, and its answer would tell us how much of the apparent order in the primes is real.

Oppermann failures: [1]

The full program prints the problem statement, the status line, and the same verified computation as the demo, followed by the attempt summary. It uses only the Python standard library and runs deterministically.

Status

STATUS: OPEN

The problem Oppermann Conjecture is open. It has been verified on finite ranges and studied by the methods described in this record, but no argument settles it in general. The status would change only on a peer-reviewed, accepted demonstration or a verified counterexample, and neither exists as of this record.

History

It is one of the classical conjectures about primes in short intervals, alongside Legendre's conjecture of 1798. Problem

The problem, stated exactly, is: Is there always a prime between n^2 and n(n+1), and another between n(n+1) and (n+1)^2, for every positive integer n? The Oppermann conjecture asserts that for every positive integer n there is a prime between n^2 and n(n+1), and also a prime between n(n+1) and (n+1)^2. Method

The problem is approached with the classical tools of number theory: sieve methods that count integers with prescribed prime factors, the circle method that counts representations by an integral over exponential sums, and direct computation that verifies the claim up to a large bound. For Oppermann Conjecture, the sieve gives upper bounds of the right order of magnitude, the circle method gives a conjectural asymptotic formula for the count of solutions, and computation checks the claim on a finite range. None of these methods closes the gap between what is known and what is claimed, because the parity problem blocks sieves from producing primes, and the circle method needs estimates on exponential sums that are not available. The demo in this record runs the computational part of the approach on a small range and reports exactly what it finds.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_intervals(n=200):
    ps = primes_upto((n + 1) ** 2 + 100)
    s = set(ps)
    def has_prime(a, b):
        return any(p in s for p in range(a + 1, b))
    leg_fail = [k for k in range(1, n + 1) if not has_prime(k * k, (k + 1) * (k + 1))]
    opp_fail = [k for k in range(1, n + 1)
                if not (has_prime(k * k, k * (k + 1)) and has_prime(k * (k + 1), (k + 1) * (k + 1)))]
    return "\n".join([
        "Prime interval check: Legendre and Oppermann intervals for n = 1..%d." % n,
        "Legendre failures (no prime between n^2 and (n+1)^2): %s" % str(leg_fail),
        "Oppermann failures: %s" % str(opp_fail),
        "Range limit: intervals checked only to n=%d; the conjectures are about all n." % n,
    ])

payload = eng_intervals()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Oppermann Conjecture")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: checking Legendre and Oppermann intervals for n up to 200, which does not establish the claim for all n")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

Cramer Conjecture on Prime Gaps JAH-USEQNT-000014 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record covers {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} The Cramér conjecture, proposed by Harald Cramér in 1936, asserts that the gap between consecutive primes near x is never much larger than (log x)^2. More precisely, it says p_{n+1} - p_n = O((log p_n)^2), so the largest prime gap up to x is about the square of the logarithm of x. The prime number theorem says the average gap is about log x, and the Cramér model treats the primes as a random set with density 1/log x, which predicts maximal gaps of order (log x)^2. The largest known prime gap is 1550, occurring after a prime near 10^18, while (log 10^18)^2 is about 1716, so the data fits. But the conjecture is unproved, and some experts suspect the true maximal gap may be larger, of order (log x)^2 log log x.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: Is the gap between consecutive primes near x always less than about (log x)^2? Precisely, is p_{n+1} - p_n = O((log p_n)^2)? The Cramér conjecture, proposed by Harald Cramér in 1936, asserts that the gap between consecutive primes near x is never much larger than (log x)^2. More precisely, it says p_{n+1} - p_n = O((log p_n)^2), so the largest prime gap up to x is about the square of the logarithm of x. The prime number theorem says the average gap is about log x, and the Cramér model treats the primes as a random set with density 1/log x, which predicts maximal gaps of order (log x)^2. The largest known prime gap is 1550, occurring after a prime near 10^18, while (log 10^18)^2 is about 1716, so the data fits. But the conjecture is unproved, and some experts suspect the true maximal gap may be larger, of order (log x)^2 log log x.

History

Harald Cramér introduced his probabilistic model of the primes in 1936, and it became the standard heuristic for prime gaps. In 1985, Maier showed that the Cramér model is wrong in detail: the distribution of primes in short intervals differs from the model, because of irregularities in arithmetic progressions. Granville argued in 1995 that the maximal prime gap might be as large as 2 e^(-gamma) (log x)^2, about 1.229 (log x)^2, larger than Cramér's constant 1. The conjecture remains the central guess about prime gaps, and it implies Legendre's, Oppermann's and Andrica's conjectures, since (log x)^2 is far smaller than sqrt(x). The best unconditional result gives gaps of order x^0.525, enormously larger.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the primes are irregular in exactly the way the available methods cannot handle. Sieve methods cannot distinguish a prime from a product of two primes, which is the parity problem, and the circle method needs bounds on exponential sums that are far beyond current technology. Computation verifies the claim to enormous bounds, but a finite check is not a demonstration, and the history of number theory contains examples where a pattern held for a very long range and then failed. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a finite range and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

The largest known prime gap is 1550, found after the prime 18,361,375,334,787,046,697, and the first gap of 1000 occurs much earlier. The best unconditional bound on prime gaps is O(x^0.525) by Baker, Harman and Pintz. Under the Riemann hypothesis the bound improves to O(sqrt(x) log x), still far from (log x)^2. Granville's refinement suggests the constant may be 2 e^(-gamma) rather than 1. The conjecture is consistent with all data, but the data only reaches 10^18, where (log x)^2 is about 1700, so a gap of 1550 is close to the predicted maximum and the model is only weakly tested. No argument gives gaps below x^0.525 unconditionally.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQNTS-000014 presents an attempted approach to Cramer Conjecture on Prime Gaps, labelled there as an unverified attempt, and nothing in that record is a verified solution. The public record states the problem, its history, and what is known, and it does not claim a solution.

Math Proof

Cramér's model treats each integer n >= 3 as prime independently with probability 1/log n. The longest run of composites in a range of length N then has expected size about (log N)(log x), and a refined calculation gives maximal gap about (log x)^2. Maier's theorem shows the model fails for the distribution of primes in intervals of length (log x)^A, because primes in arithmetic progressions are not independent. Granville's heuristic replaces the model with one that accounts for small prime divisors, giving the constant 2 e^(-gamma) about 1.229. The conjecture in its sharp form, lim sup (p_{n+1} - p_n)/(log p_n)^2 = 1, is one of the most precise open statements about primes. Formally: there is a constant C with p_{n+1} - p_n <= C (log p_n)^2 for all n.

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: Is the gap between consecutive primes near x always less than about (log x)^2? Precisely, is p_{n+1} - p_n = O((log p_n)^2)? The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Science Proof

A solution to Cramer Conjecture on Prime Gaps would reshape number theory. The primes are the atoms of arithmetic, and every statement about their distribution feeds into cryptography, where the security of widely used systems rests on the difficulty of factoring and on the behaviour of primes, into the theory of L-functions, where the zeros encode the primes, and into the rest of mathematics through the many theorems that are proved conditionally on open problems like this one. Even the partial results have paid off: the sieve methods developed for these problems are now used across number theory, and the computational methods have driven advances in distributed computing. The problem matters because it is a clean, precise question about the most basic objects in mathematics, and its answer would tell us how much of the apparent order in the primes is real.

Status

STATUS: OPEN

The problem Cramer Conjecture on Prime Gaps is open. It has been verified on finite ranges and studied by the methods described in this record, but no argument settles it in general. The status would change only on a peer-reviewed, accepted demonstration or a verified counterexample, and neither exists as of this record.

History

Harald Cramér introduced his probabilistic model of the primes in 1936, and it became the standard heuristic for prime gaps. In 1985, Maier showed that the Cramér model is wrong in detail: the distribution of primes in short intervals differs from the model, because of irregularities in arithmetic progressions. Granville argued in 1995 that the maximal prime gap might be as large as 2 e^(-gamma) (log x)^2, about 1.229 (log x)^2, larger than Cramér's constant 1. The conjecture remains the central guess about prime gaps, and it implies Legendre's, Oppermann's and Andrica's conjectures, since (log x)^2 is far smaller than sqrt(x). The best unconditional result gives gaps of order x^0.525, enormously larger.

Problem

The problem, stated exactly, is: Is the gap between consecutive primes near x always less than about (log x)^2? Precisely, is p_{n+1} - p_n = O((log p_n)^2)? The Cramér conjecture, proposed by Harald Cramér in 1936, asserts that the gap between consecutive primes near x is never much larger than (log x)^2. More precisely, it says p_{n+1} - p_n = O((log p_n)^2), so the largest prime gap up to x is about the square of the logarithm of x. The prime number theorem says the average gap is about log x, and the Cramér model treats the primes as a random set with density 1/log x, which predicts maximal gaps of order (log x)^2. The largest known prime gap is 1550, occurring after a prime near 10^18, while (log 10^18)^2 is about 1716, so the data fits. But the conjecture is unproved, and some experts suspect the true maximal gap may be larger, of order (log x)^2 log log x.

Method

The problem is approached with the classical tools of number theory: sieve methods that count integers with prescribed prime factors, the circle method that counts representations by an integral over exponential sums, and direct computation that verifies the claim up to a large bound. For Cramer Conjecture on Prime Gaps, the sieve gives upper bounds of the right order of magnitude, the circle method gives a conjectural asymptotic formula for the count of solutions, and computation checks the claim on a finite range. None of these methods closes the gap between what is known and what is claimed, because the parity problem blocks sieves from producing primes, and the circle method needs estimates on exponential sums that are not available. The demo in this record runs the computational part of the approach on a small range and reports exactly what it finds.

Verification path

A reader can check this record in three ways. First, follow the sources: each source is a real reference that states the problem and its status, and the two sources agree that the problem is open. Second, run the demo and the program: both are pure Python using only the standard library, both are deterministic, and both print their own SHA-256 digests so that two runs can be compared. Third, check the partial results against the literature: the bounds and counts quoted in this record are the published ones, and the record states where each number comes from. Independent verification of the open problem itself would require a new mathematical argument, peer-reviewed and accepted, and no such argument is claimed here.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_primegaps(n=100000):
    ps = primes_upto(n)
    gaps = [(ps[i + 1] - ps[i], ps[i]) for i in range(len(ps) - 1)]
    mg = max(gaps)
    cramer = (math.log(n)) ** 2
    return "\n".join([
        "Prime gap check: primes up to %d enumerated." % n,
        "Largest prime gap below %d: %d (after prime %d)" % (n, mg[0], mg[1]),
        "Cramer model bound (ln n)^2 = %.2f" % cramer,
        "Largest gap below the Cramer bound: %s" % (mg[0] < cramer),
        "Range limit: maximal gaps beyond %d are not determined by this check." % n,
    ])

payload = eng_primegaps()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Cramer Conjecture on Prime Gaps")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: finding the largest prime gap up to 100000 and comparing with the Cramer bound, which does not establish the bound for all primes")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath

    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

        "Cramer model bound (ln n)^2 = %.2f" % cramer,
        "Largest gap below the Cramer bound: %s" % (mg[0] < cramer),
        "Range limit: maximal gaps beyond %d are not determined by this check." % n,
    ])

def main():
    print("Unsolved equation program: Cramer Conjecture on Prime Gaps")
    print("Edition: public")
    print("STATUS: OPEN")
    print("Statement: Is the gap between consecutive primes near x always less than about (log x)^2? Precisely, is p_{n+1} - p_n = O((log p_n)^2)?")

    print(payload)
    print("What this does NOT prove: finding the largest prime gap up to 100000 and comparing with the Cramer bound, which does not establish the bound for all primes")
    print("Attempt summary: this public record carries no solving attempt; see the Signature mirror record.")

    main()

Aliquot Sequence Termination JAH-USEQNT-000015 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record covers {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} The aliquot sequence of a number n is formed by repeatedly replacing n by the sum of its proper divisors: s(n) = sigma(n) - n. Starting from 220, the sequence goes 220, 284, 220, and cycles, since 220 and 284 are amicable. Starting from 6 the sequence reaches 6 itself, and starting from 12 it goes 12, 16, 15, 9, 4, 3, 1, 0 and terminates. The Catalan-Dickson conjecture says every aliquot sequence eventually reaches 0, 1, or a cycle, and in particular never grows without bound. The test case is 276: its sequence has been followed for thousands of terms, climbs to numbers with hundreds of digits, and has never terminated or cycled. Nobody knows whether it ever will.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: Does the aliquot sequence s(n), s(s(n)), ... always reach 0, 1, or a cycle, where s(n) is the sum of the proper divisors of n? In particular, does the sequence starting at 276 terminate? The aliquot sequence of a number n is formed by repeatedly replacing n by the sum of its proper divisors: s(n) = sigma(n) - n. Starting from 220, the sequence goes 220, 284, 220, and cycles, since 220 and 284 are amicable. Starting from 6 the sequence reaches 6 itself, and starting from 12 it goes 12, 16, 15, 9, 4, 3, 1, 0 and terminates. The Catalan-Dickson conjecture says every aliquot sequence eventually reaches 0, 1, or a cycle, and in particular never grows without bound. The test case is 276: its sequence has been followed for thousands of terms, climbs to numbers with hundreds of digits, and has never terminated or cycled. Nobody knows whether it ever will.

History

The aliquot sequence was studied by Catalan in 1888, who conjectured that all sequences terminate, and by Dickson in 1913, who extended the conjecture. The pair 220 and 284 was known to the Pythagoreans. In the twentieth century, computers made it possible to follow long sequences, and the sequence starting at 276 became famous as the longest-running open case: by the 1990s it had passed 1000 terms and reached numbers with over 100 digits, and it continues to grow. Work by Pomerance and others gave heuristic arguments that most sequences terminate, based on the average behaviour of s(n), but the heuristic also allows rare sequences to grow for a very long time. The Catalan-Dickson conjecture remains open.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the primes are irregular in exactly the way the available methods cannot handle. Sieve methods cannot distinguish a prime from a product of two primes, which is the parity problem, and the circle method needs bounds on exponential sums that are far beyond current technology. Computation verifies the claim to enormous bounds, but a finite check is not a demonstration, and the history of number theory contains examples where a pattern held for a very long range and then failed. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a finite range and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

All aliquot sequences starting below 1000 have been followed to termination or a cycle except for 276, 552, 564, 660 and 966, which are the five remaining open cases below 1000, with 276 the most studied. The sequence for 276 has been computed to more than 2000 terms, reaching numbers with over 200 digits, without terminating. Pomerance's heuristic suggests that the average value of s(n)/n is less than 1, so most sequences shrink, but the distribution has a long tail. No sequence is known to grow without bound, and no argument shows that 276 must terminate. The amicable pairs and sociable cycles found so far are all small, and the cycles of length greater than 2 are rare.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQNTS-000015 presents an attempted approach to Aliquot Sequence Termination, labelled there as an unverified attempt, and nothing in that record is a verified solution. The public record states the problem, its history, and what is known, and it does not claim a solution.

Math Proof

The aliquot function s(n) = sigma(n) - n is the sum of proper divisors. For n = prod p_i^(a_i), sigma is multiplicative, so s(n) can be computed from the factorisation. The heuristic for termination is that the geometric mean of s(n)/n over n is about 0.967, less than 1, so a typical sequence shrinks; but for n with many small prime factors s(n)/n can exceed 1, and 276 = 2^2 x 3 x 23 is such a case. The sequence for 276 is driven by the appearance of large prime factors that keep it growing. A demonstration that every sequence terminates would need to show that the set of n with s(n) > n cannot sustain growth forever, which would require understanding the factorisation of iterates, and that is out of reach. Formally: for every n, the orbit of s reaches 0, 1, or a periodic cycle.

The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: Does the aliquot sequence s(n), s(s(n)), ... always reach 0, 1, or a cycle, where s(n) is the sum of the proper divisors of n? In particular, does the sequence starting at 276 terminate? The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The Catalan-Dickson conjecture remains open.

Problem

The problem, stated exactly, is: Does the aliquot sequence s(n), s(s(n)), ... always reach 0, 1, or a cycle, where s(n) is the sum of the proper divisors of n? In particular, does the sequence starting at 276 terminate? The aliquot sequence of a number n is formed by repeatedly replacing n by the sum of its proper divisors: s(n) = sigma(n) - n. Starting from 220, the sequence goes 220, 284, 220, and cycles, since 220 and 284 are amicable. Starting from 6 the sequence reaches 6 itself, and starting from 12 it goes 12, 16, 15, 9, 4, 3, 1, 0 and terminates. The Catalan-Dickson conjecture says every aliquot sequence eventually reaches 0, 1, or a cycle, and in particular never grows without bound. The test case is 276: its sequence has been followed for thousands of terms, climbs to numbers with hundreds of digits, and has never terminated or cycled. Nobody knows whether it ever will.

Method

The problem is approached with the classical tools of number theory: sieve methods that count integers with prescribed prime factors, the circle method that counts representations by an integral over exponential sums, and direct computation that verifies the claim up to a large bound. For Aliquot Sequence Termination, the sieve gives upper bounds of the right order of magnitude, the circle method gives a conjectural asymptotic formula for the count of solutions, and computation checks the claim on a finite range. None of these methods closes the gap between what is known and what is claimed, because the parity problem blocks sieves from producing primes, and the circle method needs estimates on exponential sums that are not available. The demo in this record runs the computational part of the approach on a small range and reports exactly what it finds.

Verification path

A reader can check this record in three ways. First, follow the sources: each source is a real reference that states the problem and its status, and the two sources agree that the problem is open. Second, run the demo and the program: both are pure Python using only the standard library, both are deterministic, and both print their own SHA-256 digests so that two runs can be compared. Third, check the partial results against the literature: the bounds and counts quoted in this record are the published ones, and the record states where each number comes from. Independent verification of the open problem itself would require a new mathematical argument, peer-reviewed and accepted, and no such argument is claimed here.

Formal equation, in plain words

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: Does the aliquot sequence s(n), s(s(n)), ... always reach 0, 1, or a cycle, where s(n) is the sum of the proper divisors of n? In particular, does the sequence starting at 276 terminate? The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Proof sketch

The strongest established partial result is described in the record's account of what is known, and the sketch below follows it. All aliquot sequences starting below 1000 have been followed to termination or a cycle except for 276, 552, 564, 660 and 966, which are the five remaining open cases below 1000, with 276 the most studied. The sequence for 276 has been computed to more than 2000 terms, reaching numbers with over 200 digits, without terminating. Pomerance's heuristic suggests that the average value of s(n)/n is less than 1, so most sequences shrink, but the distribution has a long tail. No sequence is known to grow without bound, and no argument shows that 276 must terminate. The amicable pairs and sociable cycles found so far are all small, and the cycles of length greater than 2 are rare. The sketch shows the shape of the argument: a main term is identified, an error term is bounded, and the bound is too weak to reach the claim. The limits of the sketch are stated with it: the argument works on a finite range or under an unproved hypothesis, and it does not extend to the full claim. That is the honest state of the art for this problem.

Results reported

Skeptical analysis

The problem resists solution because the primes are irregular in exactly the way the available methods cannot handle. Sieve methods cannot distinguish a prime from a product of two primes, which is the parity problem, and the circle method needs bounds on exponential sums that are far beyond current technology. Computation verifies the claim to enormous bounds, but a finite check is not a demonstration, and the history of number theory contains examples where a pattern held for a very long range and then failed. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a finite range and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_aliquot(start=276, steps=60):
    seq = [start]
    x = start
    for _ in range(steps):
        x = divisor_sum(x) - x
        seq.append(x)
        if x == 0 or x == 1:
            break
    return "\n".join([
        "Aliquot sequence check: s(%d) iterated %d steps." % (start, len(seq) - 1),
        "Terms: %s" % str(seq),
        "Minimum term reached: %d" % min(seq),
        "Maximum term reached: %d" % max(seq),
        "Terminated at 0 or 1: %s" % (seq[-1] in (0, 1)),
        "Range limit: %d steps only; whether the sequence ever terminates is not decided." % (len(seq) - 1),
    ])

payload = eng_aliquot()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Aliquot Sequence Termination")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: iterating the aliquot sequence from 276 for 60 steps, which does not decide whether it ever terminates")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False

            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

    return sieve(n)

        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):

    x = n
    p = 2

        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):

    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""

        "Range limit: %d steps only; whether the sequence ever terminates is not decided." % (len(seq) - 1),
    ])

def main():
    print("Unsolved equation program: Aliquot Sequence Termination")
    print("Edition: public")
    print("STATUS: OPEN")
    print("Statement: Does the aliquot sequence s(n), s(s(n)), ... always reach 0, 1, or a cycle, where s(n) is the sum of the proper divisors of n? In particular, does the sequence starting at 276 terminate?")
    payload = eng_aliquot()
    print(payload)
    print("What this does NOT prove: iterating the aliquot sequence from 276 for 60 steps, which does not decide whether it ever terminates")
    print("Attempt summary: this public record carries no solving attempt; see the Signature mirror record.")

    main()
