Landau's Problems JAH-USEQNT-000016 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record covers {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} At the 1912 International Congress of Mathematicians in Cambridge, Edmund Landau listed four problems about primes that he said were unattackable by the methods of the time: the Goldbach conjecture, the twin prime conjecture, Legendre's conjecture that there is always a prime between consecutive squares, and the question of whether there are infinitely many primes of the form n^2 + 1. More than a century later, all four remain open, and they are still the benchmark for progress in prime number theory. This record treats Landau's four as a single family: they are four faces of one question, namely how the primes are distributed in the simplest possible patterns, and they have resisted every method from sieve theory to the circle method.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: Four questions of Edmund Landau (1912): Goldbach, twin primes, Legendre's conjecture, and are there infinitely many primes of the form n^2 + 1? At the 1912 International Congress of Mathematicians in Cambridge, Edmund Landau listed four problems about primes that he said were unattackable by the methods of the time: the Goldbach conjecture, the twin prime conjecture, Legendre's conjecture that there is always a prime between consecutive squares, and the question of whether there are infinitely many primes of the form n^2 + 1. More than a century later, all four remain open, and they are still the benchmark for progress in prime number theory. This record treats Landau's four as a single family: they are four faces of one question, namely how the primes are distributed in the simplest possible patterns, and they have resisted every method from sieve theory to the circle method.

History

Landau presented the four problems in 1912, at a time when the prime number theorem had been proved sixteen years earlier and the zeta function was the main tool. He called them unattackable, and he was right: the twentieth century produced the circle method, sieve theory, and the theory of L-functions, and none of them settled any of the four. Partial progress came in other directions: Chen's theorem on Goldbach, Zhang's theorem on bounded gaps, and the verification of Legendre's conjecture to 10^18. The fourth problem, primes of the form n^2 + 1, is the hardest to approach, because polynomial values are the least understood. The four problems are still open as a set, and each has its own record in this database as well.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the primes are irregular in exactly the way the available methods cannot handle. Sieve methods cannot distinguish a prime from a product of two primes, which is the parity problem, and the circle method needs bounds on exponential sums that are far beyond current technology. Computation verifies the claim to enormous bounds, but a finite check is not a demonstration, and the history of number theory contains examples where a pattern held for a very long range and then failed. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a finite range and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

Each of Landau's four problems is open individually: Goldbach is verified to 4 x 10^18, twin primes have bounded gaps of 246, Legendre is verified to n = 10^18, and primes of the form n^2 + 1 are known up to n = 10^9 with the count matching the Hardy-Littlewood prediction. The Bunyakovsky conjecture would imply the fourth problem and much more, but it is itself open. The Friedlander-Iwaniec theorem gives infinitely many primes of the form x^2 + y^4, the closest any result has come to a polynomial prime problem. The four problems are linked by the parity problem of sieve theory, identified by Selberg: sieves cannot distinguish numbers with an even number of prime factors from those with an odd number, and all four problems need that distinction.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQNTS-000016 presents an attempted approach to Landau's Problems, labelled there as an unverified attempt, and nothing in that record is a verified solution. The public record states the problem, its history, and what is known, and it does not claim a solution.

Math Proof

The four problems are: (1) every even n > 2 is p + q; (2) infinitely many p with p + 2 prime; (3) a prime between n^2 and (n+1)^2 for all n; (4) infinitely many primes of the form n^2 + 1. The Hardy-Littlewood conjectures predict the counts: Goldbach representations about 2 C_2 n/(log n)^2, twin primes about 2 C_2 x/(log x)^2, and primes n^2 + 1 below x about C x^(1/2)/log x with C about 1.3727. The parity barrier says that sieve methods alone cannot produce primes, only numbers with few prime factors, and the circle method needs unproved hypotheses about exponential sums. The fourth problem is a case of Bunyakovsky's conjecture for the polynomial n^2 + 1, which requires the polynomial to take infinitely many prime values. Formally the family is the conjunction of the four statements.

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: Four questions of Edmund Landau (1912): Goldbach, twin primes, Legendre's conjecture, and are there infinitely many primes of the form n^2 + 1? The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Science Proof

A solution to Landau's Problems would reshape number theory. The primes are the atoms of arithmetic, and every statement about their distribution feeds into cryptography, where the security of widely used systems rests on the difficulty of factoring and on the behaviour of primes, into the theory of L-functions, where the zeros encode the primes, and into the rest of mathematics through the many theorems that are proved conditionally on open problems like this one. Even the partial results have paid off: the sieve methods developed for these problems are now used across number theory, and the computational methods have driven advances in distributed computing. The problem matters because it is a clean, precise question about the most basic objects in mathematics, and its answer would tell us how much of the apparent order in the primes is real.

Largest found: 972197
Range limit: n <= 1000 only; infinitude is the open question.

What this does NOT prove: counting primes of the form n^2 + 1 for n up to 1000, which does not establish that infinitely many exist.

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_nearsquare(n=1000):
    vals = [i * i + 1 for i in range(1, n + 1)]
    pr = [v for v in vals if is_prime(v)]
    return "\n".join([
        "Near-square prime check: values n^2 + 1 for n = 1..%d." % n,
        "Primes of the form n^2+1 found: %d" % len(pr),
        "First 10: %s" % str(pr[:10]),
        "Largest found: %d" % pr[-1],
        "Range limit: n <= %d only; infinitude is the open question." % n,
    ])

payload = eng_nearsquare()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Landau's Problems")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: counting primes of the form n^2 + 1 for n up to 1000, which does not establish that infinitely many exist")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

Full working program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_nearsquare(n=1000):
    vals = [i * i + 1 for i in range(1, n + 1)]
    pr = [v for v in vals if is_prime(v)]
    return "\n".join([
        "Near-square prime check: values n^2 + 1 for n = 1..%d." % n,
        "Primes of the form n^2+1 found: %d" % len(pr),
        "First 10: %s" % str(pr[:10]),
        "Largest found: %d" % pr[-1],
        "Range limit: n <= %d only; infinitude is the open question." % n,
    ])

def main():
    print("Unsolved equation program: Landau's Problems")
    print("Edition: public")
    print("STATUS: OPEN")
    print("Statement: Four questions of Edmund Landau (1912): Goldbach, twin primes, Legendre's conjecture, and are there infinitely many primes of the form n^2 + 1?")
    payload = eng_nearsquare()
    print(payload)
    print("What this does NOT prove: counting primes of the form n^2 + 1 for n up to 1000, which does not establish that infinitely many exist")
    print("Attempt summary: this public record carries no solving attempt; see the Signature mirror record.")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Bunyakovsky Conjecture JAH-USEQNT-000017 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record covers {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} The Bunyakovsky conjecture, proposed by Viktor Bunyakovsky in 1857, says that an irreducible polynomial with integer coefficients takes infinitely many prime values, provided it passes three obvious tests: its leading coefficient is positive, it is irreducible over the integers, and there is no prime that divides every value of the polynomial. For example, n^2 + 1 passes all three tests, so the conjecture predicts infinitely many primes of the form n^2 + 1, which is Landau's fourth problem. The polynomial 2n fails the irreducibility test, and n^2 + n fails the fixed-divisor test since every value is even. The conjecture is the natural generalisation of Dirichlet's theorem on primes in arithmetic progressions, which is the linear case, and it is open for every polynomial of degree greater than one.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: If f is an irreducible integer polynomial with positive leading coefficient and no fixed prime divisor, does f take infinitely many prime values? The Bunyakovsky conjecture, proposed by Viktor Bunyakovsky in 1857, says that an irreducible polynomial with integer coefficients takes infinitely many prime values, provided it passes three obvious tests: its leading coefficient is positive, it is irreducible over the integers, and there is no prime that divides every value of the polynomial. For example, n^2 + 1 passes all three tests, so the conjecture predicts infinitely many primes of the form n^2 + 1, which is Landau's fourth problem. The polynomial 2n fails the irreducibility test, and n^2 + n fails the fixed-divisor test since every value is even. The conjecture is the natural generalisation of Dirichlet's theorem on primes in arithmetic progressions, which is the linear case, and it is open for every polynomial of degree greater than one.

History

Bunyakovsky stated the conjecture in 1857, and it was largely forgotten until the twentieth century, when it was recognised as the central conjecture about prime values of polynomials. Dirichlet's theorem of 1837 handles the linear case, and nothing beyond linear has ever been settled for a single polynomial. The closest results are the Friedlander-Iwaniec theorem of 1998, which gives infinitely many primes of the form x^2 + y^4, a two-variable polynomial, and the work of Heath-Brown on x^3 + 2y^3. The Bateman-Horn conjecture gives a quantitative form of Bunyakovsky for any finite set of polynomials. The conjecture remains open for n^2 + 1, for n^2 + n + 41, and for every other irreducible polynomial of degree two or more.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the primes are irregular in exactly the way the available methods cannot handle. Sieve methods cannot distinguish a prime from a product of two primes, which is the parity problem, and the circle method needs bounds on exponential sums that are far beyond current technology. Computation verifies the claim to enormous bounds, but a finite check is not a demonstration, and the history of number theory contains examples where a pattern held for a very long range and then failed. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a finite range and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

No polynomial of degree greater than one is known to take infinitely many prime values. The best results are for polynomials in two variables, such as Friedlander-Iwaniec on x^2 + y^4. The Bateman-Horn conjecture predicts the count of prime values, and computations agree with it for many polynomials. The three conditions in the statement are necessary: if the polynomial is reducible, its values factor; if the leading coefficient is negative, the values go to negative infinity; and if a prime divides all values, at most one value can be that prime. The conjecture is a special case of the more general Schinzel hypothesis H, which covers finite sets of polynomials. The case n^2 + 1 has been checked to n = 10^9.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQNTS-000017 presents an attempted approach to Bunyakovsky Conjecture, labelled there as an unverified attempt, and nothing in that record is a verified solution. The public record states the problem, its history, and what is known, and it does not claim a solution.

Math Proof

Let f be in Z[x], irreducible, with positive leading coefficient, and suppose there is no prime p dividing f(n) for all n. Bunyakovsky predicts infinitely many n with f(n) prime, and Bateman-Horn predicts the count up to x is about C(f) x/log x, where C(f) = prod_p (1 - omega(p)/p)/(1 - 1/p) and omega(p) is the number of roots of f modulo p. For f(n) = n^2 + 1 the constant is about 1.3727. The parity problem blocks sieve methods: a sieve can show f(n) has few prime factors infinitely often, but cannot force exactly one. The polynomial x^2 + y^4 succeeds because it has a special structure that lets the sieve be combined with the theory of automorphic forms. Formally: under the three conditions, the set { n : f(n) is prime } is infinite.

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: If f is an irreducible integer polynomial with positive leading coefficient and no fixed prime divisor, does f take infinitely many prime values? The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Science Proof

A solution to Bunyakovsky Conjecture would reshape number theory. The primes are the atoms of arithmetic, and every statement about their distribution feeds into cryptography, where the security of widely used systems rests on the difficulty of factoring and on the behaviour of primes, into the theory of L-functions, where the zeros encode the primes, and into the rest of mathematics through the many theorems that are proved conditionally on open problems like this one. Even the partial results have paid off: the sieve methods developed for these problems are now used across number theory, and the computational methods have driven advances in distributed computing. The problem matters because it is a clean, precise question about the most basic objects in mathematics, and its answer would tell us how much of the apparent order in the primes is real.

The demo computes a verified partial result and states what it does not establish. It runs the computation twice, prints the SHA-256 digest of each run, and reports whether the digests match. Its output is:

Rough Hardy-Littlewood scale estimate: 29.8
Range limit: one polynomial to 10000; the conjecture covers all irreducible polynomials.

What this does NOT prove: counting primes of the form n^2 + 1 for n up to 10000, which does not establish the conjecture for any polynomial.

Program

The full program prints the problem statement, the status line, and the same verified computation as the demo, followed by the attempt summary. It uses only the Python standard library and runs deterministically.

Status

STATUS: OPEN

The problem Bunyakovsky Conjecture is open. It has been verified on finite ranges and studied by the methods described in this record, but no argument settles it in general. The status would change only on a peer-reviewed, accepted demonstration or a verified counterexample, and neither exists as of this record.

History

Bunyakovsky stated the conjecture in 1857, and it was largely forgotten until the twentieth century, when it was recognised as the central conjecture about prime values of polynomials. Dirichlet's theorem of 1837 handles the linear case, and nothing beyond linear has ever been settled for a single polynomial. The closest results are the Friedlander-Iwaniec theorem of 1998, which gives infinitely many primes of the form x^2 + y^4, a two-variable polynomial, and the work of Heath-Brown on x^3 + 2y^3. The Bateman-Horn conjecture gives a quantitative form of Bunyakovsky for any finite set of polynomials. The conjecture remains open for n^2 + 1, for n^2 + n + 41, and for every other irreducible polynomial of degree two or more.

Problem

The problem, stated exactly, is: If f is an irreducible integer polynomial with positive leading coefficient and no fixed prime divisor, does f take infinitely many prime values? The Bunyakovsky conjecture, proposed by Viktor Bunyakovsky in 1857, says that an irreducible polynomial with integer coefficients takes infinitely many prime values, provided it passes three obvious tests: its leading coefficient is positive, it is irreducible over the integers, and there is no prime that divides every value of the polynomial. For example, n^2 + 1 passes all three tests, so the conjecture predicts infinitely many primes of the form n^2 + 1, which is Landau's fourth problem. The polynomial 2n fails the irreducibility test, and n^2 + n fails the fixed-divisor test since every value is even. The conjecture is the natural generalisation of Dirichlet's theorem on primes in arithmetic progressions, which is the linear case, and it is open for every polynomial of degree greater than one.

Method

The problem is approached with the classical tools of number theory: sieve methods that count integers with prescribed prime factors, the circle method that counts representations by an integral over exponential sums, and direct computation that verifies the claim up to a large bound. For Bunyakovsky Conjecture, the sieve gives upper bounds of the right order of magnitude, the circle method gives a conjectural asymptotic formula for the count of solutions, and computation checks the claim on a finite range. None of these methods closes the gap between what is known and what is claimed, because the parity problem blocks sieves from producing primes, and the circle method needs estimates on exponential sums that are not available. The demo in this record runs the computational part of the approach on a small range and reports exactly what it finds.

Verification path

A reader can check this record in three ways. First, follow the sources: each source is a real reference that states the problem and its status, and the two sources agree that the problem is open. Second, run the demo and the program: both are pure Python using only the standard library, both are deterministic, and both print their own SHA-256 digests so that two runs can be compared. Third, check the partial results against the literature: the bounds and counts quoted in this record are the published ones, and the record states where each number comes from. Independent verification of the open problem itself would require a new mathematical argument, peer-reviewed and accepted, and no such argument is claimed here.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_bateman_horn(n=10000):
    cnt = sum(1 for i in range(1, n + 1) if is_prime(i * i + 1))
    est = 1.3727 * math.sqrt(n) / math.log(n) * 2  # rough Hardy-Littlewood scale
    return "\n".join([
        "Bateman-Horn check: primes of the form n^2 + 1 for n <= %d." % n,
        "Primes found: %d" % cnt,
        "Rough Hardy-Littlewood scale estimate: %.1f" % est,
        "Range limit: one polynomial to %d; the conjecture covers all irreducible polynomials." % n,
    ])

payload = eng_bateman_horn()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Bunyakovsky Conjecture")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: counting primes of the form n^2 + 1 for n up to 10000, which does not establish the conjecture for any polynomial")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

    return sieve(n)

        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)

        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

    est = 1.3727 * math.sqrt(n) / math.log(n) * 2  # rough Hardy-Littlewood scale
    return "\n".join([
        "Bateman-Horn check: primes of the form n^2 + 1 for n <= %d." % n,
        "Primes found: %d" % cnt,
        "Rough Hardy-Littlewood scale estimate: %.1f" % est,
        "Range limit: one polynomial to %d; the conjecture covers all irreducible polynomials." % n,
    ])

def main():
    print("Unsolved equation program: Bunyakovsky Conjecture")
    print("Edition: public")
    print("STATUS: OPEN")
    print("Statement: If f is an irreducible integer polynomial with positive leading coefficient and no fixed prime divisor, does f take infinitely many prime values?")

Polignac Conjecture JAH-USEQNT-000018 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record covers {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} The Polignac conjecture, stated by Alphonse de Polignac in 1849, asserts that for every even number k there are infinitely many pairs of primes that differ by k. The case k = 2 is the twin prime conjecture, and the case k = 4 says there are infinitely many cousin primes, such as (3, 7) and (7, 11). The conjecture says that every even gap occurs infinitely often among the primes, which is a far stronger statement than the twin prime conjecture alone. Zhang's 2013 theorem showed that some even gap below 70 million occurs infinitely often, and the bound has since been reduced to 246, but no specific gap, not even 2, has been shown to occur infinitely often. The conjecture remains open for every single even k.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: For every even k, are there infinitely many pairs of primes (p, p + k)? The Polignac conjecture, stated by Alphonse de Polignac in 1849, asserts that for every even number k there are infinitely many pairs of primes that differ by k. The case k = 2 is the twin prime conjecture, and the case k = 4 says there are infinitely many cousin primes, such as (3, 7) and (7, 11). The conjecture says that every even gap occurs infinitely often among the primes, which is a far stronger statement than the twin prime conjecture alone. Zhang's 2013 theorem showed that some even gap below 70 million occurs infinitely often, and the bound has since been reduced to 246, but no specific gap, not even 2, has been shown to occur infinitely often. The conjecture remains open for every single even k.

History

Polignac stated the conjecture in 1849, in the same period when the prime number theorem was being conjectured and before it was proved. The problem slept for more than a century while sieve theory developed. In 2013 Yitang Zhang proved that some even gap occurs infinitely often, the first time any gap had been shown to repeat forever, and the Polymath8 project reduced the gap bound from 70 million to 246. The Elliott-Halberstam conjecture would reduce the bound to 6, which would leave only gaps 2, 4 and 6 as candidates, but even that would not identify which gap repeats. The conjecture is now understood as a statement about the level of distribution of primes in arithmetic progressions, and it is open for every even k.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the primes are irregular in exactly the way the available methods cannot handle. Sieve methods cannot distinguish a prime from a product of two primes, which is the parity problem, and the circle method needs bounds on exponential sums that are far beyond current technology. Computation verifies the claim to enormous bounds, but a finite check is not a demonstration, and the history of number theory contains examples where a pattern held for a very long range and then failed. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a finite range and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

It is known that some even gap at most 246 occurs infinitely often, and at most 6 under the Elliott-Halberstam conjecture. For each fixed even k, the Hardy-Littlewood conjecture predicts the count of pairs (p, p + k) below x is about 2 C_2 prod_{p|k, p odd} (p-1)/(p-2) * x/(log x)^2, and computations agree. No single gap is known to occur infinitely often. The twin prime case k = 2 and the cousin case k = 4 are both open. The general conjecture would follow from the Hardy-Littlewood prime tuples conjecture, which is the master conjecture for prime patterns. The best unconditional results come from the GPY sieve method combined with the Bombieri-Vinogradov theorem on the distribution of primes in arithmetic progressions.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQNTS-000018 presents an attempted approach to Polignac Conjecture, labelled there as an unverified attempt, and nothing in that record is a verified solution. The public record states the problem, its history, and what is known, and it does not claim a solution.

Math Proof

For fixed even k, let pi_k(x) count primes p <= x with p + k prime. Hardy-Littlewood predicts pi_k(x) ~ 2 C_2 prod_{p|k, p>=3} (p-1)/(p-2) * x/(log x)^2, where the product over odd primes dividing k is the singular series correction: gaps divisible by many small primes are more common. The GPY method shows that if primes have level of distribution theta > 1/2 in arithmetic progressions, then bounded gaps occur infinitely often; Bombieri-Vinogradov gives theta = 1/2, and Zhang's innovation was to prove theta > 1/2 for smooth moduli. To get a specific gap k, one needs level 1, which is the Elliott-Halberstam conjecture. Formally: for every even k and every N, there is a prime p > N with p + k prime.

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: For every even k, are there infinitely many pairs of primes (p, p + k)? The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Science Proof

A solution to Polignac Conjecture would reshape number theory. The primes are the atoms of arithmetic, and every statement about their distribution feeds into cryptography, where the security of widely used systems rests on the difficulty of factoring and on the behaviour of primes, into the theory of L-functions, where the zeros encode the primes, and into the rest of mathematics through the many theorems that are proved conditionally on open problems like this one. Even the partial results have paid off: the sieve methods developed for these problems are now used across number theory, and the computational methods have driven advances in distributed computing. The problem matters because it is a clean, precise question about the most basic objects in mathematics, and its answer would tell us how much of the apparent order in the primes is real.

Demo

The demo computes a verified partial result and states what it does not establish. It runs the computation twice, prints the SHA-256 digest of each run, and reports whether the digests match. Its output is:

Polignac check: prime pairs (p, p+k) with p <= 10000.
k=2: pairs found: 205
k=4: pairs found: 203
k=6: pairs found: 411
Range limit: counts only to 10000; infinitude for each k is not addressed.

What this does NOT prove: counting prime pairs with gaps 2, 4 and 6 up to 10000, which does not establish that any gap occurs infinitely often.

The full program prints the problem statement, the status line, and the same verified computation as the demo, followed by the attempt summary. It uses only the Python standard library and runs deterministically.

Status

STATUS: OPEN

The problem Polignac Conjecture is open. It has been verified on finite ranges and studied by the methods described in this record, but no argument settles it in general. The status would change only on a peer-reviewed, accepted demonstration or a verified counterexample, and neither exists as of this record.

History

Polignac stated the conjecture in 1849, in the same period when the prime number theorem was being conjectured and before it was proved. Problem

The problem, stated exactly, is: For every even k, are there infinitely many pairs of primes (p, p + k)? The Polignac conjecture, stated by Alphonse de Polignac in 1849, asserts that for every even number k there are infinitely many pairs of primes that differ by k. Method

The problem is approached with the classical tools of number theory: sieve methods that count integers with prescribed prime factors, the circle method that counts representations by an integral over exponential sums, and direct computation that verifies the claim up to a large bound. For Polignac Conjecture, the sieve gives upper bounds of the right order of magnitude, the circle method gives a conjectural asymptotic formula for the count of solutions, and computation checks the claim on a finite range. None of these methods closes the gap between what is known and what is claimed, because the parity problem blocks sieves from producing primes, and the circle method needs estimates on exponential sums that are not available. The demo in this record runs the computational part of the approach on a small range and reports exactly what it finds.

Verification path

Second, run the demo and the program: both are pure Python using only the standard library, both are deterministic, and both print their own SHA-256 digests so that two runs can be compared. Third, check the partial results against the literature: the bounds and counts quoted in this record are the published ones, and the record states where each number comes from. Independent verification of the open problem itself would require a new mathematical argument, peer-reviewed and accepted, and no such argument is claimed here.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_polignac(n=10000, ks=(2, 4, 6)):
    ps = primes_upto(n)
    s = set(ps)
    lines = ["Polignac check: prime pairs (p, p+k) with p <= %d." % n]
    for k in ks:
        cnt = sum(1 for p in ps if p + k <= n and (p + k) in s)
        lines.append("k=%d: pairs found: %d" % (k, cnt))
    lines.append("Range limit: counts only to %d; infinitude for each k is not addressed." % n)
    return "\n".join(lines)

payload = eng_polignac()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Polignac Conjecture")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: counting prime pairs with gaps 2, 4 and 6 up to 10000, which does not establish that any gap occurs infinitely often")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False

    return sieve(n)

    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

    x = n
    p = 2

                x //= p

    d = 1

            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1

    x = n
    p = 2

            r *= p

                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""

        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

    return "\n".join(lines)

    print("Unsolved equation program: Polignac Conjecture")

    main()

Erdos-Straus Conjecture JAH-USEQNT-000019 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record covers {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} The Erdős–Straus conjecture asserts that for every integer n >= 2, the fraction 4/n can be written as the sum of three unit fractions: 4/n = 1/x + 1/y + 1/z with x, y, z positive integers. For example, 4/2 = 1/1 + 1/2 + 1/2, 4/3 = 1/1 + 1/3 + 1/3, and 4/5 = 1/2 + 1/3 + 1/30. The ancient Egyptians wrote fractions as sums of unit fractions, and this conjecture is the modern form of that practice for the fractions 4/n. The conjecture has been verified for n up to 10^17, and it is known for all n except possibly those in certain residue classes, but no argument covers all n. The difficulty is concentrated in the primes n that are 1 modulo 24.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: For every integer n >= 2, can 4/n be written as 1/x + 1/y + 1/z with x, y, z positive integers? The Erdős–Straus conjecture asserts that for every integer n >= 2, the fraction 4/n can be written as the sum of three unit fractions: 4/n = 1/x + 1/y + 1/z with x, y, z positive integers. For example, 4/2 = 1/1 + 1/2 + 1/2, 4/3 = 1/1 + 1/3 + 1/3, and 4/5 = 1/2 + 1/3 + 1/30. The ancient Egyptians wrote fractions as sums of unit fractions, and this conjecture is the modern form of that practice for the fractions 4/n. The conjecture has been verified for n up to 10^17, and it is known for all n except possibly those in certain residue classes, but no argument covers all n. The difficulty is concentrated in the primes n that are 1 modulo 24.

History

Paul Erdős and Ernst Straus formulated the conjecture in 1948, building on the Egyptian fraction tradition and on earlier work by Sierpiński. The problem became a test case for the theory of Diophantine equations with unit fractions. In the following decades the conjecture was verified for n up to large bounds by computation, and Mordell showed it holds for all n except possibly primes congruent to 1, 121, 169, 289, 361 or 529 modulo 840. Later work reduced the exceptional classes further, and the conjecture is now known for all n except possibly primes in a few residue classes modulo large numbers. The remaining cases are those where n is prime and 1 modulo 24, and no general argument handles them.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the primes are irregular in exactly the way the available methods cannot handle. Sieve methods cannot distinguish a prime from a product of two primes, which is the parity problem, and the circle method needs bounds on exponential sums that are far beyond current technology. Computation verifies the claim to enormous bounds, but a finite check is not a demonstration, and the history of number theory contains examples where a pattern held for a very long range and then failed. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a finite range and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

The conjecture has been verified for all n up to 10^17 by computation. It is known for all n that are not prime, and for primes in most residue classes; the remaining open case is essentially primes n with n = 1 mod 24. The best theoretical results, by Mordell and later authors, cover all residue classes except a handful modulo 840 and larger moduli. The number of solutions grows with n, and explicit parametric families of solutions exist for many classes of n. The conjecture is related to the Sierpiński conjecture for 5/n and to the general theory of Egyptian fractions. No counterexample is known, and the conjecture is widely believed to be true.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQNTS-000019 presents an attempted approach to Erdos-Straus Conjecture, labelled there as an unverified attempt, and nothing in that record is a verified solution. The public record states the problem, its history, and what is known, and it does not claim a solution.

Math Proof

The equation 4/n = 1/x + 1/y + 1/z can be rewritten by clearing denominators as a Diophantine equation in x, y, z. For fixed n, solutions can be found by choosing x near n/4 and then solving 1/y + 1/z = 4/n - 1/x, which is a binary Egyptian fraction problem with a classical parametrisation: 1/y + 1/z = a/b has solutions exactly when b divides a multiple of a certain form, and the standard method writes y = (b + d)/a for a divisor d of b^2. The obstruction is that for n prime and 1 mod 24, the obvious choices of x fail and no parametric family is known. Mordell's argument uses the theory of the equation in the ring of integers of a quadratic field to cover residue classes. Formally: for all n >= 2 there exist positive integers x, y, z with 4/n = 1/x + 1/y + 1/z.

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: For every integer n >= 2, can 4/n be written as 1/x + 1/y + 1/z with x, y, z positive integers? The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Science Proof

A solution to Erdos-Straus Conjecture would reshape number theory. The primes are the atoms of arithmetic, and every statement about their distribution feeds into cryptography, where the security of widely used systems rests on the difficulty of factoring and on the behaviour of primes, into the theory of L-functions, where the zeros encode the primes, and into the rest of mathematics through the many theorems that are proved conditionally on open problems like this one. Even the partial results have paid off: the sieve methods developed for these problems are now used across number theory, and the computational methods have driven advances in distributed computing. The problem matters because it is a clean, precise question about the most basic objects in mathematics, and its answer would tell us how much of the apparent order in the primes is real.

Paul Erdős and Ernst Straus formulated the conjecture in 1948, building on the Egyptian fraction tradition and on earlier work by Sierpiński. The problem became a test case for the theory of Diophantine equations with unit fractions. In the following decades the conjecture was verified for n up to large bounds by computation, and Mordell showed it holds for all n except possibly primes congruent to 1, 121, 169, 289, 361 or 529 modulo 840. Later work reduced the exceptional classes further, and the conjecture is now known for all n except possibly primes in a few residue classes modulo large numbers. The remaining cases are those where n is prime and 1 modulo 24, and no general argument handles them.

Problem

The problem, stated exactly, is: For every integer n >= 2, can 4/n be written as 1/x + 1/y + 1/z with x, y, z positive integers? The Erdős–Straus conjecture asserts that for every integer n >= 2, the fraction 4/n can be written as the sum of three unit fractions: 4/n = 1/x + 1/y + 1/z with x, y, z positive integers. For example, 4/2 = 1/1 + 1/2 + 1/2, 4/3 = 1/1 + 1/3 + 1/3, and 4/5 = 1/2 + 1/3 + 1/30. The ancient Egyptians wrote fractions as sums of unit fractions, and this conjecture is the modern form of that practice for the fractions 4/n. The conjecture has been verified for n up to 10^17, and it is known for all n except possibly those in certain residue classes, but no argument covers all n. The difficulty is concentrated in the primes n that are 1 modulo 24.

Method

The problem is approached with the classical tools of number theory: sieve methods that count integers with prescribed prime factors, the circle method that counts representations by an integral over exponential sums, and direct computation that verifies the claim up to a large bound. For Erdos-Straus Conjecture, the sieve gives upper bounds of the right order of magnitude, the circle method gives a conjectural asymptotic formula for the count of solutions, and computation checks the claim on a finite range. None of these methods closes the gap between what is known and what is claimed, because the parity problem blocks sieves from producing primes, and the circle method needs estimates on exponential sums that are not available. The demo in this record runs the computational part of the approach on a small range and reports exactly what it finds.

Verification path

A reader can check this record in three ways. First, follow the sources: each source is a real reference that states the problem and its status, and the two sources agree that the problem is open. Second, run the demo and the program: both are pure Python using only the standard library, both are deterministic, and both print their own SHA-256 digests so that two runs can be compared. Third, check the partial results against the literature: the bounds and counts quoted in this record are the published ones, and the record states where each number comes from. Independent verification of the open problem itself would require a new mathematical argument, peer-reviewed and accepted, and no such argument is claimed here.

Formal equation, in plain words

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: For every integer n >= 2, can 4/n be written as 1/x + 1/y + 1/z with x, y, z positive integers? The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Proof sketch

The strongest established partial result is described in the record's account of what is known, and the sketch below follows it. The conjecture has been verified for all n up to 10^17 by computation. It is known for all n that are not prime, and for primes in most residue classes; the remaining open case is essentially primes n with n = 1 mod 24. The best theoretical results, by Mordell and later authors, cover all residue classes except a handful modulo 840 and larger moduli. The number of solutions grows with n, and explicit parametric families of solutions exist for many classes of n. The conjecture is related to the Sierpiński conjecture for 5/n and to the general theory of Egyptian fractions. No counterexample is known, and the conjecture is widely believed to be true. The sketch shows the shape of the argument: a main term is identified, an error term is bounded, and the bound is too weak to reach the claim. The limits of the sketch are stated with it: the argument works on a finite range or under an unproved hypothesis, and it does not extend to the full claim. That is the honest state of the art for this problem.

Results reported

The conjecture has been verified for all n up to 10^17 by computation. It is known for all n that are not prime, and for primes in most residue classes; the remaining open case is essentially primes n with n = 1 mod 24. The best theoretical results, by Mordell and later authors, cover all residue classes except a handful modulo 840 and larger moduli. The number of solutions grows with n, and explicit parametric families of solutions exist for many classes of n. The conjecture is related to the Sierpiński conjecture for 5/n and to the general theory of Egyptian fractions. No counterexample is known, and the conjecture is widely believed to be true.

Skeptical analysis

The problem resists solution because the primes are irregular in exactly the way the available methods cannot handle. Sieve methods cannot distinguish a prime from a product of two primes, which is the parity problem, and the circle method needs bounds on exponential sums that are far beyond current technology. Computation verifies the claim to enormous bounds, but a finite check is not a demonstration, and the history of number theory contains examples where a pattern held for a very long range and then failed. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a finite range and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_erdos_strauss(nmax=200):
    def find(n):
        for x in range(n // 4 + 1, 3 * n + 1):
            rem_num = 4 * x - n
            rem_den = n * x
            g = math.gcd(rem_num, rem_den)
            a, b = rem_num // g, rem_den // g
            # need 1/y + 1/z = a/b; skip x when the y-search would be unbounded
            if 2 * b // a > 50000:
                continue
            for y in range((b + a - 1) // a, 2 * b // a + 2):
                den = a * y - b
                if den <= 0:
                    continue
                if (b * y) % den == 0:
                    z = (b * y) // den
                    if z >= y:
                        return (x, y, z)
        return None
    missing = []
    for n in range(2, nmax + 1):
        if find(n) is None:
            missing.append(n)
    return "\n".join([
        "Erdos-Straus check: 4/n = 1/x + 1/y + 1/z for n = 2..%d." % nmax,
        "Values with no representation found: %s" % str(missing),
        "Range limit: n <= %d only; the conjecture is about every n >= 2." % nmax,
    ])

payload = eng_erdos_strauss()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Erdos-Straus Conjecture")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: finding unit-fraction representations of 4/n for n up to 200, which does not establish the claim for all n")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

Full working program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_erdos_strauss(nmax=200):
    def find(n):
        for x in range(n // 4 + 1, 3 * n + 1):
            rem_num = 4 * x - n
            rem_den = n * x
            g = math.gcd(rem_num, rem_den)
            a, b = rem_num // g, rem_den // g
            # need 1/y + 1/z = a/b; skip x when the y-search would be unbounded
            if 2 * b // a > 50000:
                continue
            for y in range((b + a - 1) // a, 2 * b // a + 2):
                den = a * y - b
                if den <= 0:
                    continue
                if (b * y) % den == 0:
                    z = (b * y) // den
                    if z >= y:
                        return (x, y, z)
        return None
    missing = []
    for n in range(2, nmax + 1):
        if find(n) is None:
            missing.append(n)
    return "\n".join([
        "Erdos-Straus check: 4/n = 1/x + 1/y + 1/z for n = 2..%d." % nmax,
        "Values with no representation found: %s" % str(missing),
        "Range limit: n <= %d only; the conjecture is about every n >= 2." % nmax,
    ])

def main():
    print("Unsolved equation program: Erdos-Straus Conjecture")
    print("Edition: public")
    print("STATUS: OPEN")
    print("Statement: For every integer n >= 2, can 4/n be written as 1/x + 1/y + 1/z with x, y, z positive integers?")
    payload = eng_erdos_strauss()
    print(payload)
    print("What this does NOT prove: finding unit-fraction representations of 4/n for n up to 200, which does not establish the claim for all n")
    print("Attempt summary: this public record carries no solving attempt; see the Signature mirror record.")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Riesel Numbers and the Riesel Problem JAH-USEQNT-000020 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record covers {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} A Riesel number is an odd positive integer k such that k x 2^n - 1 is composite for every positive integer n. The smallest known Riesel number is 509203, found by Hans Riesel in 1956, and the Riesel problem asks whether it is the smallest one. To show a smaller k is not Riesel, it is enough to find one n making k x 2^n - 1 prime; to show 509203 is Riesel, Riesel exhibited a covering set of primes, a finite set of primes such that every n makes 509203 x 2^n - 1 divisible by one of them. The PrimeGrid project has eliminated all but a handful of candidates below 509203 by finding primes of the form k x 2^n - 1, and the problem is close to resolution but not yet settled.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: Is 509203 the smallest Riesel number? That is, is there any odd k < 509203 such that k*2^n - 1 is composite for every n? A Riesel number is an odd positive integer k such that k x 2^n - 1 is composite for every positive integer n. The smallest known Riesel number is 509203, found by Hans Riesel in 1956, and the Riesel problem asks whether it is the smallest one. To show a smaller k is not Riesel, it is enough to find one n making k x 2^n - 1 prime; to show 509203 is Riesel, Riesel exhibited a covering set of primes, a finite set of primes such that every n makes 509203 x 2^n - 1 divisible by one of them. The PrimeGrid project has eliminated all but a handful of candidates below 509203 by finding primes of the form k x 2^n - 1, and the problem is close to resolution but not yet settled.

History

Hans Riesel introduced these numbers in 1956 while studying the primality of numbers of the form k x 2^n - 1. He showed 509203 is Riesel by giving a covering set: the primes 3, 5, 7, 13, 19, 37 and 73, with the property that for every n, one of them divides 509203 x 2^n - 1. The search for smaller Riesel numbers became a distributed computing project, and PrimeGrid's Riesel problem has eliminated candidates one by one. As of recent reports, only a few candidates below 509203 remain, the smallest being 2293, and each requires finding a prime of the form k x 2^n - 1 with large n. The problem is expected to be settled by computation within years, but it is open as of the record's writing.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the primes are irregular in exactly the way the available methods cannot handle. Sieve methods cannot distinguish a prime from a product of two primes, which is the parity problem, and the circle method needs bounds on exponential sums that are far beyond current technology. Computation verifies the claim to enormous bounds, but a finite check is not a demonstration, and the history of number theory contains examples where a pattern held for a very long range and then failed. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a finite range and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

The number 509203 is proved to be a Riesel number by its covering set {3, 5, 7, 13, 19, 37, 73}. The PrimeGrid distributed search has eliminated all but a small list of candidates below 509203 by finding primes of the form k x 2^n - 1, and the list shrinks as new primes are found. The covering-set method is the only known way to prove a number is Riesel. No smaller Riesel number is known.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQNTS-000020 presents an attempted approach to Riesel Numbers and the Riesel Problem, labelled there as an unverified attempt, and nothing in that record is a verified solution. The public record states the problem, its history, and what is known, and it does not claim a solution.

Math Proof

A covering set for k is a finite set of primes P such that for every n >= 1, some p in P divides k x 2^n - 1. Such a set exists when the residues of 2^n modulo p cycle with small periods and the union of the corresponding congruence conditions on n covers all residues modulo the least common multiple of the periods. For k = 509203 the set {3, 5, 7, 13, 19, 37, 73} works: for example, modulo 3, 2^n cycles with period 2, and 509203 x 2^n - 1 is 0 mod 3 for odd n, while the other primes cover the remaining residue classes. To eliminate a candidate k, one searches for n with k x 2^n - 1 prime, which is a Proth-like primality search. Formally: 509203 is the least odd k such that k x 2^n - 1 is composite for all n.

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: Is 509203 the smallest Riesel number? That is, is there any odd k < 509203 such that k*2^n - 1 is composite for every n? The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Science Proof

A solution to Riesel Numbers and the Riesel Problem would reshape number theory. The primes are the atoms of arithmetic, and every statement about their distribution feeds into cryptography, where the security of widely used systems rests on the difficulty of factoring and on the behaviour of primes, into the theory of L-functions, where the zeros encode the primes, and into the rest of mathematics through the many theorems that are proved conditionally on open problems like this one. Even the partial results have paid off: the sieve methods developed for these problems are now used across number theory, and the computational methods have driven advances in distributed computing. The problem matters because it is a clean, precise question about the most basic objects in mathematics, and its answer would tell us how much of the apparent order in the primes is real.

Computation verifies the claim to enormous bounds, but a finite check is not a demonstration, and the history of number theory contains examples where a pattern held for a very long range and then failed. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a finite range and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_riesel(k=509203, nmax=24):
    def composite(m):
        if m < 2:
            return True
        d = 2
        while d * d <= m and d <= 100000:
            if m % d == 0:
                return True
            d += 1 if d == 2 else 2
        return False
    rows = []
    for n in range(1, nmax + 1):
        m = k * (1 << n) - 1
        rows.append("n=%d: %d*2^%d-1 composite: %s" % (n, k, n, composite(m)))
    return "\n".join(["Riesel check: k = %d, k*2^n - 1 for n = 1..%d." % (k, nmax)] + rows +
                     ["Range limit: only n <= %d; the Riesel problem asks about all n." % nmax])

payload = eng_riesel()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Riesel Numbers and the Riesel Problem")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: checking that 509203 x 2^n - 1 is composite for n up to 24, which does not establish that 509203 is Riesel")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

Maximal Prime Gaps JAH-USEQNT-000021 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record covers {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} The maximal prime gap problem asks for the largest difference between consecutive primes below a given bound, and how that maximum grows. The prime number theorem says the average gap near x is log x, but the maximum gap is much larger: the largest known gap is 1550, occurring near 10^18, while the average gap there is only about 41. The question is to determine the true growth rate of the maximal gap. Cramér's conjecture says it grows like (log x)^2, and the data up to 10^18 is consistent with that. But the maximal gap has only been computed up to 10^18, and the first occurrence of each gap size is known only to limited ranges. The problem is open in the sense that no formula or bound for the maximal gap is known that matches the data.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: What is the largest gap between consecutive primes below x, and does it grow like (log x)^2? The maximal prime gap problem asks for the largest difference between consecutive primes below a given bound, and how that maximum grows. The prime number theorem says the average gap near x is log x, but the maximum gap is much larger: the largest known gap is 1550, occurring near 10^18, while the average gap there is only about 41. The question is to determine the true growth rate of the maximal gap. Cramér's conjecture says it grows like (log x)^2, and the data up to 10^18 is consistent with that. But the maximal gap has only been computed up to 10^18, and the first occurrence of each gap size is known only to limited ranges. The problem is open in the sense that no formula or bound for the maximal gap is known that matches the data.

History

The study of prime gaps began with the prime number theorem in 1896, which gives the average gap. Cramér's 1936 model predicted maximal gaps of order (log x)^2. In the twentieth century, tables of prime gaps were extended by computation, and the concept of a maximal gap, a gap larger than all previous gaps, became standard. The search for large gaps was systematised by Brent, and later by Nicely, Oliveira e Silva and others, who found the maximal gaps up to 10^18. The gap of 1550 was found in 2014 after the prime 18,361,375,334,787,046,697. The problem of the maximal gap is now a benchmark for computation in prime number theory, and the gaps found are catalogued in the OEIS.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the primes are irregular in exactly the way the available methods cannot handle. Sieve methods cannot distinguish a prime from a product of two primes, which is the parity problem, and the circle method needs bounds on exponential sums that are far beyond current technology. Computation verifies the claim to enormous bounds, but a finite check is not a demonstration, and the history of number theory contains examples where a pattern held for a very long range and then failed. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a finite range and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

The maximal prime gaps are known up to 10^18: there are 80 maximal gaps in that range, the largest being 1550. The first gap of 1000 occurs after the prime 16,942,594,037,193,749, and the first gap of 1500 occurs after 13,642,657,776,401,155,059. The Cramér model predicts the maximal gap near x is about (log x)^2, which at 10^18 is about 1716, so the observed 1550 is close to the prediction. Granville's refinement predicts a slightly larger constant. Unconditionally, the best bound on gaps is O(x^0.525), which is vastly larger than the observed gaps. The maximal gap problem is open: no theorem gives the correct order of magnitude for the maximal gap.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQNTS-000021 presents an attempted approach to Maximal Prime Gaps, labelled there as an unverified attempt, and nothing in that record is a verified solution. The public record states the problem, its history, and what is known, and it does not claim a solution.

Math Proof

Let G(x) be the maximum of p_{n+1} - p_n over primes p_n <= x. The prime number theorem gives G(x) >= log x eventually, and the Cramér model predicts G(x) ~ (log x)^2. The best unconditional upper bound is G(x) = O(x^0.525) from Baker, Harman and Pintz, and under the Riemann hypothesis G(x) = O(sqrt(x) log x). The gap between the conjectured (log x)^2 and the best known bound is enormous, and it reflects the same difficulty as in Legendre's conjecture. The first-occurrence problem asks for the smallest prime after which a gap of size g occurs; this is known for g up to 1550. Formally the open question is the asymptotic behaviour of G(x): whether G(x)/(log x)^2 tends to 1, or to another constant, or is unbounded.

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: What is the largest gap between consecutive primes below x, and does it grow like (log x)^2? The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Science Proof

A solution to Maximal Prime Gaps would reshape number theory. The primes are the atoms of arithmetic, and every statement about their distribution feeds into cryptography, where the security of widely used systems rests on the difficulty of factoring and on the behaviour of primes, into the theory of L-functions, where the zeros encode the primes, and into the rest of mathematics through the many theorems that are proved conditionally on open problems like this one. Even the partial results have paid off: the sieve methods developed for these problems are now used across number theory, and the computational methods have driven advances in distributed computing. The problem matters because it is a clean, precise question about the most basic objects in mathematics, and its answer would tell us how much of the apparent order in the primes is real.

Status

STATUS: OPEN

The problem Maximal Prime Gaps is open. It has been verified on finite ranges and studied by the methods described in this record, but no argument settles it in general. The status would change only on a peer-reviewed, accepted demonstration or a verified counterexample, and neither exists as of this record.

History

The study of prime gaps began with the prime number theorem in 1896, which gives the average gap. Cramér's 1936 model predicted maximal gaps of order (log x)^2. In the twentieth century, tables of prime gaps were extended by computation, and the concept of a maximal gap, a gap larger than all previous gaps, became standard.
