Problem

The problem, stated exactly, is: What is the largest gap between consecutive primes below x, and does it grow like (log x)^2? The maximal prime gap problem asks for the largest difference between consecutive primes below a given bound, and how that maximum grows. The prime number theorem says the average gap near x is log x, but the maximum gap is much larger: the largest known gap is 1550, occurring near 10^18, while the average gap there is only about 41. The question is to determine the true growth rate of the maximal gap. Cramér's conjecture says it grows like (log x)^2, and the data up to 10^18 is consistent with that. But the maximal gap has only been computed up to 10^18, and the first occurrence of each gap size is known only to limited ranges. The problem is open in the sense that no formula or bound for the maximal gap is known that matches the data.

Method

The problem is approached with the classical tools of number theory: sieve methods that count integers with prescribed prime factors, the circle method that counts representations by an integral over exponential sums, and direct computation that verifies the claim up to a large bound. For Maximal Prime Gaps, the sieve gives upper bounds of the right order of magnitude, the circle method gives a conjectural asymptotic formula for the count of solutions, and computation checks the claim on a finite range. None of these methods closes the gap between what is known and what is claimed, because the parity problem blocks sieves from producing primes, and the circle method needs estimates on exponential sums that are not available. The demo in this record runs the computational part of the approach on a small range and reports exactly what it finds.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_primegaps(n=100000):
    ps = primes_upto(n)
    gaps = [(ps[i + 1] - ps[i], ps[i]) for i in range(len(ps) - 1)]
    mg = max(gaps)
    cramer = (math.log(n)) ** 2
    return "\n".join([
        "Prime gap check: primes up to %d enumerated." % n,
        "Largest prime gap below %d: %d (after prime %d)" % (n, mg[0], mg[1]),
        "Cramer model bound (ln n)^2 = %.2f" % cramer,
        "Largest gap below the Cramer bound: %s" % (mg[0] < cramer),
        "Range limit: maximal gaps beyond %d are not determined by this check." % n,
    ])

payload = eng_primegaps()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Maximal Prime Gaps")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: finding the largest prime gap up to 100000, which does not determine the growth of maximal gaps")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_primegaps(n=100000):
    ps = primes_upto(n)
    gaps = [(ps[i + 1] - ps[i], ps[i]) for i in range(len(ps) - 1)]
    mg = max(gaps)
    cramer = (math.log(n)) ** 2
    return "\n".join([
        "Prime gap check: primes up to %d enumerated." % n,
        "Largest prime gap below %d: %d (after prime %d)" % (n, mg[0], mg[1]),
        "Cramer model bound (ln n)^2 = %.2f" % cramer,
        "Largest gap below the Cramer bound: %s" % (mg[0] < cramer),
        "Range limit: maximal gaps beyond %d are not determined by this check." % n,
    ])

def main():
    print("Unsolved equation program: Maximal Prime Gaps")
    print("Edition: public")
    print("STATUS: OPEN")
    print("Statement: What is the largest gap between consecutive primes below x, and does it grow like (log x)^2?")
    payload = eng_primegaps()
    print(payload)
    print("What this does NOT prove: finding the largest prime gap up to 100000, which does not determine the growth of maximal gaps")
    print("Attempt summary: this public record carries no solving attempt; see the Signature mirror record.")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Lehmer Totient Problem JAH-USEQNT-000022 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record covers {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} The Lehmer totient problem asks whether there is a composite number n such that Euler's totient function phi(n) divides n - 1. For prime n, phi(n) = n - 1, so the divisibility holds trivially; the question is whether any composite n shares the property. Lehmer asked the question in 1932, and it is still open. It is known that any such n, called a Lehmer number, would have to be odd, squarefree, and divisible by at least 7 primes, and would have to exceed 10^30. The problem is connected with the theory of Carmichael numbers and with the search for pseudoprimes, and it is one of the classic open problems about the totient function.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: Is there a composite n such that phi(n) divides n - 1? The Lehmer totient problem asks whether there is a composite number n such that Euler's totient function phi(n) divides n - 1. For prime n, phi(n) = n - 1, so the divisibility holds trivially; the question is whether any composite n shares the property. Lehmer asked the question in 1932, and it is still open. It is known that any such n, called a Lehmer number, would have to be odd, squarefree, and divisible by at least 7 primes, and would have to exceed 10^30. The problem is connected with the theory of Carmichael numbers and with the search for pseudoprimes, and it is one of the classic open problems about the totient function.

History

Derrick Lehmer posed the problem in 1932 in a paper on the totient function. The problem attracted attention because phi(n) = n - 1 characterises the primes, and the question asks whether the characterisation is sharp. In the following decades, bounds were proved one by one: a Lehmer number must be odd, must be squarefree, must have at least 7 distinct prime factors, and must be larger than 10^30. Work by Pomerance, and later by others, connected the problem with the distribution of Carmichael numbers. The problem remains open, and the bounds continue to improve slowly as computation and theory advance. No Lehmer number has ever been found.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the primes are irregular in exactly the way the available methods cannot handle. Sieve methods cannot distinguish a prime from a product of two primes, which is the parity problem, and the circle method needs bounds on exponential sums that are far beyond current technology. Computation verifies the claim to enormous bounds, but a finite check is not a demonstration, and the history of number theory contains examples where a pattern held for a very long range and then failed. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a finite range and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

Any Lehmer number must be odd, squarefree, divisible by at least 7 distinct primes, and greater than 10^30. The bound on the number of prime factors comes from the inequality phi(n) <= n prod_{p|n} (1 - 1/p), which forces many small prime factors if phi(n) divides n - 1. The bound 10^30 comes from combining the factor count with the requirement that n - 1 is a multiple of phi(n) and that n is squarefree. It is also known that if n is a Lehmer number then n must be a Carmichael number, and the smallest Carmichael numbers are far below the bound, so the connection does not produce an example. The problem is open: no composite n with phi(n) | n - 1 is known, and no argument rules one out.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQNTS-000022 presents an attempted approach to Lehmer Totient Problem, labelled there as an unverified attempt, and nothing in that record is a verified solution. The public record states the problem, its history, and what is known, and it does not claim a solution.

Math Proof

For n = prod p_i with distinct primes, phi(n) = n prod (1 - 1/p_i). The condition phi(n) | n - 1 means n - 1 = k phi(n) for some integer k, and since phi(n) < n, k >= 1. If n has r distinct prime factors, then n/phi(n) = prod p_i/(p_i - 1), and this product is at most the product over the first r primes, which bounds r from below once k is bounded. The requirement that n is squarefree follows because if p^2 divides n then p divides phi(n) and also divides n - 1, which is impossible. The oddness follows similarly from 2 dividing phi(n) for n > 2. Formally: there is no composite n with phi(n) | (n - 1), or there is one, and the problem asks which.

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: Is there a composite n such that phi(n) divides n - 1? The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Science Proof

A solution to Lehmer Totient Problem would reshape number theory. The primes are the atoms of arithmetic, and every statement about their distribution feeds into cryptography, where the security of widely used systems rests on the difficulty of factoring and on the behaviour of primes, into the theory of L-functions, where the zeros encode the primes, and into the rest of mathematics through the many theorems that are proved conditionally on open problems like this one. Even the partial results have paid off: the sieve methods developed for these problems are now used across number theory, and the computational methods have driven advances in distributed computing. The problem matters because it is a clean, precise question about the most basic objects in mathematics, and its answer would tell us how much of the apparent order in the primes is real.

The status would change only on a peer-reviewed, accepted demonstration or a verified counterexample, and neither exists as of this record.

The problem, stated exactly, is: Is there a composite n such that phi(n) divides n - 1? The Lehmer totient problem asks whether there is a composite number n such that Euler's totient function phi(n) divides n - 1. For prime n, phi(n) = n - 1, so the divisibility holds trivially; the question is whether any composite n shares the property. Lehmer asked the question in 1932, and it is still open. The problem is connected with the theory of Carmichael numbers and with the search for pseudoprimes, and it is one of the classic open problems about the totient function.

Method

The problem is approached with the classical tools of number theory: sieve methods that count integers with prescribed prime factors, the circle method that counts representations by an integral over exponential sums, and direct computation that verifies the claim up to a large bound. For Lehmer Totient Problem, the sieve gives upper bounds of the right order of magnitude, the circle method gives a conjectural asymptotic formula for the count of solutions, and computation checks the claim on a finite range. None of these methods closes the gap between what is known and what is claimed, because the parity problem blocks sieves from producing primes, and the circle method needs estimates on exponential sums that are not available. The demo in this record runs the computational part of the approach on a small range and reports exactly what it finds.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_lehmer(n=5000):
    hits = [m for m in range(2, n + 1) if not is_prime(m) and (m - 1) % phi(m) == 0]
    return "\n".join([
        "Lehmer totient check: composite n <= %d with phi(n) dividing n-1." % n,
        "Such n found: %s" % str(hits),
        "Count: %d" % len(hits),
        "Range limit: absence below %d does not show that no such n exists." % n,
    ])

payload = eng_lehmer()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Lehmer Totient Problem")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: searching for composite n up to 5000 with phi(n) dividing n - 1, which does not establish that none exists")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

Near-Square Primes JAH-USEQNT-000023 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record covers {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} The near-square primes are the primes of the form n^2 + 1: 2, 5, 17, 37, 101, 197, 257, 401, and so on. The question is whether there are infinitely many of them. It is the fourth of Landau's problems and a special case of the Bunyakovsky conjecture for the polynomial n^2 + 1. The Hardy-Littlewood conjecture predicts about 1.3727 sqrt(x)/log x such primes below x, and the count up to 10^9 matches that prediction closely. Yet no argument shows that even one more prime of this form exists beyond those already found, let alone infinitely many. The problem is the simplest open question about prime values of a polynomial, and it has resisted every method.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: Are there infinitely many primes of the form n^2 + 1? The near-square primes are the primes of the form n^2 + 1: 2, 5, 17, 37, 101, 197, 257, 401, and so on. The question is whether there are infinitely many of them. It is the fourth of Landau's problems and a special case of the Bunyakovsky conjecture for the polynomial n^2 + 1. The Hardy-Littlewood conjecture predicts about 1.3727 sqrt(x)/log x such primes below x, and the count up to 10^9 matches that prediction closely. Yet no argument shows that even one more prime of this form exists beyond those already found, let alone infinitely many. The problem is the simplest open question about prime values of a polynomial, and it has resisted every method.

History

The problem of primes of the form n^2 + 1 goes back to Euler and was listed by Landau in 1912 as one of four unattackable problems. In the twentieth century, the Hardy-Littlewood conjectures gave a precise prediction for the count, and sieve methods showed that n^2 + 1 takes infinitely many values with at most two prime factors. The Friedlander-Iwaniec theorem of 1998 gave infinitely many primes of the form x^2 + y^4, which is the closest any result has come to a polynomial prime problem, but it does not cover n^2 + 1. The search for large primes of the form n^2 + 1 continues, and the largest known has millions of digits. The infinitude question is completely open.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the primes are irregular in exactly the way the available methods cannot handle. Sieve methods cannot distinguish a prime from a product of two primes, which is the parity problem, and the circle method needs bounds on exponential sums that are far beyond current technology. Computation verifies the claim to enormous bounds, but a finite check is not a demonstration, and the history of number theory contains examples where a pattern held for a very long range and then failed. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a finite range and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

Primes of the form n^2 + 1 have been counted up to n = 10^9, and the count matches the Hardy-Littlewood prediction with constant about 1.3727. It is known that n^2 + 1 is prime infinitely often if one allows the values to have at most two prime factors, by the work of Iwaniec, but the case of exactly one prime factor is open. The largest known prime of the form n^2 + 1 has over a million digits. The polynomial n^2 + 1 has no fixed prime divisor, is irreducible, and has positive leading coefficient, so it passes all three tests of Bunyakovsky's conjecture. No argument is known that would produce infinitely many prime values of any irreducible quadratic polynomial.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQNTS-000023 presents an attempted approach to Near-Square Primes, labelled there as an unverified attempt, and nothing in that record is a verified solution. The public record states the problem, its history, and what is known, and it does not claim a solution.

Math Proof

Let pi_{n^2+1}(x) count primes of the form n^2 + 1 with n^2 + 1 <= x. The Hardy-Littlewood prediction is pi_{n^2+1}(x) ~ C sqrt(x)/log x with C = prod_{p} (1 - chi(p)/(p-1)) about 1.3727, where chi is the character modulo 4, the product being over odd primes. The constant comes from the local densities: for a prime p = 1 mod 4, the congruence n^2 = -1 mod p has two solutions, so n^2 + 1 is divisible by p for a fraction 2/p of n, while for p = 3 mod 4 it is never divisible. Sieve methods give an upper bound of the right order, and a lower bound for values with at most two prime factors. Formally: the set { n : n^2 + 1 is prime } is infinite.

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: Are there infinitely many primes of the form n^2 + 1? The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Science Proof

A solution to Near-Square Primes would reshape number theory. The primes are the atoms of arithmetic, and every statement about their distribution feeds into cryptography, where the security of widely used systems rests on the difficulty of factoring and on the behaviour of primes, into the theory of L-functions, where the zeros encode the primes, and into the rest of mathematics through the many theorems that are proved conditionally on open problems like this one. Even the partial results have paid off: the sieve methods developed for these problems are now used across number theory, and the computational methods have driven advances in distributed computing. The problem matters because it is a clean, precise question about the most basic objects in mathematics, and its answer would tell us how much of the apparent order in the primes is real.

Status

STATUS: OPEN

The problem Near-Square Primes is open. It has been verified on finite ranges and studied by the methods described in this record, but no argument settles it in general. The status would change only on a peer-reviewed, accepted demonstration or a verified counterexample, and neither exists as of this record.

History

The problem of primes of the form n^2 + 1 goes back to Euler and was listed by Landau in 1912 as one of four unattackable problems. In the twentieth century, the Hardy-Littlewood conjectures gave a precise prediction for the count, and sieve methods showed that n^2 + 1 takes infinitely many values with at most two prime factors. The Friedlander-Iwaniec theorem of 1998 gave infinitely many primes of the form x^2 + y^4, which is the closest any result has come to a polynomial prime problem, but it does not cover n^2 + 1. The search for large primes of the form n^2 + 1 continues, and the largest known has millions of digits. The infinitude question is completely open.

Problem

The problem, stated exactly, is: Are there infinitely many primes of the form n^2 + 1? The near-square primes are the primes of the form n^2 + 1: 2, 5, 17, 37, 101, 197, 257, 401, and so on. The question is whether there are infinitely many of them. It is the fourth of Landau's problems and a special case of the Bunyakovsky conjecture for the polynomial n^2 + 1. The Hardy-Littlewood conjecture predicts about 1.3727 sqrt(x)/log x such primes below x, and the count up to 10^9 matches that prediction closely. Yet no argument shows that even one more prime of this form exists beyond those already found, let alone infinitely many. The problem is the simplest open question about prime values of a polynomial, and it has resisted every method.

Method

The problem is approached with the classical tools of number theory: sieve methods that count integers with prescribed prime factors, the circle method that counts representations by an integral over exponential sums, and direct computation that verifies the claim up to a large bound. For Near-Square Primes, the sieve gives upper bounds of the right order of magnitude, the circle method gives a conjectural asymptotic formula for the count of solutions, and computation checks the claim on a finite range. None of these methods closes the gap between what is known and what is claimed, because the parity problem blocks sieves from producing primes, and the circle method needs estimates on exponential sums that are not available. The demo in this record runs the computational part of the approach on a small range and reports exactly what it finds.

Verification path

A reader can check this record in three ways. First, follow the sources: each source is a real reference that states the problem and its status, and the two sources agree that the problem is open. Second, run the demo and the program: both are pure Python using only the standard library, both are deterministic, and both print their own SHA-256 digests so that two runs can be compared. Third, check the partial results against the literature: the bounds and counts quoted in this record are the published ones, and the record states where each number comes from. Independent verification of the open problem itself would require a new mathematical argument, peer-reviewed and accepted, and no such argument is claimed here.

Formal equation, in plain words

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: Are there infinitely many primes of the form n^2 + 1? The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Proof sketch

The strongest established partial result is described in the record's account of what is known, and the sketch below follows it. Primes of the form n^2 + 1 have been counted up to n = 10^9, and the count matches the Hardy-Littlewood prediction with constant about 1.3727. It is known that n^2 + 1 is prime infinitely often if one allows the values to have at most two prime factors, by the work of Iwaniec, but the case of exactly one prime factor is open. The largest known prime of the form n^2 + 1 has over a million digits. The polynomial n^2 + 1 has no fixed prime divisor, is irreducible, and has positive leading coefficient, so it passes all three tests of Bunyakovsky's conjecture. No argument is known that would produce infinitely many prime values of any irreducible quadratic polynomial. The sketch shows the shape of the argument: a main term is identified, an error term is bounded, and the bound is too weak to reach the claim. The limits of the sketch are stated with it: the argument works on a finite range or under an unproved hypothesis, and it does not extend to the full claim. That is the honest state of the art for this problem.

Results reported

Primes of the form n^2 + 1 have been counted up to n = 10^9, and the count matches the Hardy-Littlewood prediction with constant about 1.3727. It is known that n^2 + 1 is prime infinitely often if one allows the values to have at most two prime factors, by the work of Iwaniec, but the case of exactly one prime factor is open. The largest known prime of the form n^2 + 1 has over a million digits. The polynomial n^2 + 1 has no fixed prime divisor, is irreducible, and has positive leading coefficient, so it passes all three tests of Bunyakovsky's conjecture. No argument is known that would produce infinitely many prime values of any irreducible quadratic polynomial.

Skeptical analysis

The problem resists solution because the primes are irregular in exactly the way the available methods cannot handle. Sieve methods cannot distinguish a prime from a product of two primes, which is the parity problem, and the circle method needs bounds on exponential sums that are far beyond current technology. Computation verifies the claim to enormous bounds, but a finite check is not a demonstration, and the history of number theory contains examples where a pattern held for a very long range and then failed. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a finite range and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_nearsquare(n=1000):
    vals = [i * i + 1 for i in range(1, n + 1)]
    pr = [v for v in vals if is_prime(v)]
    return "\n".join([
        "Near-square prime check: values n^2 + 1 for n = 1..%d." % n,
        "Primes of the form n^2+1 found: %d" % len(pr),
        "First 10: %s" % str(pr[:10]),
        "Largest found: %d" % pr[-1],
        "Range limit: n <= %d only; infinitude is the open question." % n,
    ])

payload = eng_nearsquare()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Near-Square Primes")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: counting primes of the form n^2 + 1 for n up to 1000, which does not establish that infinitely many exist")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

Full working program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_nearsquare(n=1000):
    vals = [i * i + 1 for i in range(1, n + 1)]
    pr = [v for v in vals if is_prime(v)]
    return "\n".join([
        "Near-square prime check: values n^2 + 1 for n = 1..%d." % n,
        "Primes of the form n^2+1 found: %d" % len(pr),
        "First 10: %s" % str(pr[:10]),
        "Largest found: %d" % pr[-1],
        "Range limit: n <= %d only; infinitude is the open question." % n,
    ])

def main():
    print("Unsolved equation program: Near-Square Primes")
    print("Edition: public")
    print("STATUS: OPEN")
    print("Statement: Are there infinitely many primes of the form n^2 + 1?")
    payload = eng_nearsquare()
    print(payload)
    print("What this does NOT prove: counting primes of the form n^2 + 1 for n up to 1000, which does not establish that infinitely many exist")
    print("Attempt summary: this public record carries no solving attempt; see the Signature mirror record.")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Sums of Three Cubes JAH-USEQNT-000024 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record covers {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} The sums of three cubes problem asks which integers n can be written as x^3 + y^3 + z^3 with x, y, z integers, possibly negative. Numbers congruent to 4 or 5 modulo 9 are impossible, since cubes are 0, 1 or -1 modulo 9. The question is whether every other integer is a sum of three cubes. For most n the answer is yes, often with small solutions, but some numbers resisted for decades: 33 was settled in 2019 by Booker, with a solution involving numbers with 16 digits, and 42 was settled later that year by Booker and Sutherland, using a distributed search. The remaining question is whether every n not congruent to 4 or 5 modulo 9 has a representation, and the smallest unsolved cases are now in the hundreds.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: For which integers n can n = x^3 + y^3 + z^3 with x, y, z integers? Are all n not congruent to 4 or 5 modulo 9 representable as a sum of three integer cubes? The sums of three cubes problem asks which integers n can be written as x^3 + y^3 + z^3 with x, y, z integers, possibly negative. Numbers congruent to 4 or 5 modulo 9 are impossible, since cubes are 0, 1 or -1 modulo 9. The question is whether every other integer is a sum of three cubes. For most n the answer is yes, often with small solutions, but some numbers resisted for decades: 33 was settled in 2019 by Booker, with a solution involving numbers with 16 digits, and 42 was settled later that year by Booker and Sutherland, using a distributed search. The remaining question is whether every n not congruent to 4 or 5 modulo 9 has a representation, and the smallest unsolved cases are now in the hundreds.

History

The problem was studied by Miller and Woollett in the 1950s, who searched for representations by computer. Heath-Brown conjectured in 1992 that every n not 4 or 5 mod 9 is a sum of three cubes, with infinitely many representations, and gave a heuristic for the density of solutions. The case n = 33 resisted until 2019, when Andrew Booker found a solution using a new algorithm based on the theory of the equation modulo large primes, and the case n = 42 fell months later to a Charity Engine distributed search. The smallest number whose status is still unknown changes as searches advance, and the conjecture that all admissible n are representable remains open.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the primes are irregular in exactly the way the available methods cannot handle. Sieve methods cannot distinguish a prime from a product of two primes, which is the parity problem, and the circle method needs bounds on exponential sums that are far beyond current technology. Computation verifies the claim to enormous bounds, but a finite check is not a demonstration, and the history of number theory contains examples where a pattern held for a very long range and then failed. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a finite range and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

All n up to 100 except 33 and 42 were known to be sums of three cubes before 2019; 33 and 42 were settled in 2019, and the search has since extended to n up to 1000, with only a few cases remaining. The solutions for 33 and 42 involve integers with 16 and 17 digits, found by searching arithmetic progressions modulo large numbers. Heath-Brown's conjecture predicts that every n not 4 or 5 mod 9 is a sum of three cubes, and that the number of representations grows like a power of the search bound. The modular obstruction mod 9 is the only known obstruction. The problem is open: no n not 4 or 5 mod 9 is known to be impossible, and no argument shows all of them are possible.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQNTS-000024 presents an attempted approach to Sums of Three Cubes, labelled there as an unverified attempt, and nothing in that record is a verified solution. The public record states the problem, its history, and what is known, and it does not claim a solution.

Math Proof

The equation x^3 + y^3 + z^3 = n is a cubic Diophantine equation. Modulo 9, cubes are 0, +1 or -1, so a sum of three cubes is never 4 or 5 mod 9, and those n are impossible. For other n, the circle method predicts many representations: the number of solutions with |x|, |y|, |z| <= B is about C(n) B for a constant C(n) given by a singular series, so solutions are expected to be plentiful but possibly very large. Booker's algorithm for n = 33 used the parametrisation of the equation modulo a prime to restrict the search to an arithmetic progression, reducing the search space by orders of magnitude. The remaining difficulty is that the smallest solution for a given n can be enormous, and no method bounds its size. Formally: for all n not 4 or 5 mod 9, there exist integers x, y, z with x^3 + y^3 + z^3 = n.

The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: For which integers n can n = x^3 + y^3 + z^3 with x, y, z integers? Are all n not congruent to 4 or 5 modulo 9 representable as a sum of three integer cubes? Third, check the partial results against the literature: the bounds and counts quoted in this record are the published ones, and the record states where each number comes from. Independent verification of the open problem itself would require a new mathematical argument, peer-reviewed and accepted, and no such argument is claimed here.

Formal equation, in plain words

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: For which integers n can n = x^3 + y^3 + z^3 with x, y, z integers? Are all n not congruent to 4 or 5 modulo 9 representable as a sum of three integer cubes? The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Proof sketch

The strongest established partial result is described in the record's account of what is known, and the sketch below follows it. All n up to 100 except 33 and 42 were known to be sums of three cubes before 2019; 33 and 42 were settled in 2019, and the search has since extended to n up to 1000, with only a few cases remaining. The solutions for 33 and 42 involve integers with 16 and 17 digits, found by searching arithmetic progressions modulo large numbers. Heath-Brown's conjecture predicts that every n not 4 or 5 mod 9 is a sum of three cubes, and that the number of representations grows like a power of the search bound. The modular obstruction mod 9 is the only known obstruction. The problem is open: no n not 4 or 5 mod 9 is known to be impossible, and no argument shows all of them are possible. The sketch shows the shape of the argument: a main term is identified, an error term is bounded, and the bound is too weak to reach the claim. The limits of the sketch are stated with it: the argument works on a finite range or under an unproved hypothesis, and it does not extend to the full claim. That is the honest state of the art for this problem.

Results reported

All n up to 100 except 33 and 42 were known to be sums of three cubes before 2019; 33 and 42 were settled in 2019, and the search has since extended to n up to 1000, with only a few cases remaining. The solutions for 33 and 42 involve integers with 16 and 17 digits, found by searching arithmetic progressions modulo large numbers. Heath-Brown's conjecture predicts that every n not 4 or 5 mod 9 is a sum of three cubes, and that the number of representations grows like a power of the search bound. The modular obstruction mod 9 is the only known obstruction. The problem is open: no n not 4 or 5 mod 9 is known to be impossible, and no argument shows all of them are possible.

Skeptical analysis

The problem resists solution because the primes are irregular in exactly the way the available methods cannot handle. Sieve methods cannot distinguish a prime from a product of two primes, which is the parity problem, and the circle method needs bounds on exponential sums that are far beyond current technology. Computation verifies the claim to enormous bounds, but a finite check is not a demonstration, and the history of number theory contains examples where a pattern held for a very long range and then failed. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a finite range and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

Boundless scales

  • A2: Vary the presentation of the verified partial result for Sums of Three Cubes.
  • A3: Vary the worked examples and the historical detail for Sums of Three Cubes.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_cubes(nmax=32, bound=60):
    cubes = {}
    for x in range(-bound, bound + 1):
        cubes[x ** 3] = x
    lines = ["Sums of three cubes check: x^3+y^3+z^3 = n for n <= %d, |x|,|y|,|z| <= %d." % (nmax, bound)]
    missing = []
    for n in range(1, nmax + 1):
        if n % 9 in (4, 5):
            lines.append("n=%d: impossible mod 9 (n = 4 or 5 mod 9)" % n)
            continue
        found = None
        for x in range(-bound, bound + 1):
            for y in range(-bound, bound + 1):
                z3 = n - x ** 3 - y ** 3
                if z3 in cubes:
                    found = (x, y, cubes[z3])
                    break
            if found:
                break
        if found:
            lines.append("n=%d: %d^3 + %d^3 + %d^3" % (n, found[0], found[1], found[2]))
        else:
            missing.append(n)
            lines.append("n=%d: no representation with |x|,|y|,|z| <= %d" % (n, bound))
    lines.append("Values with no small representation: %s" % str(missing))
    lines.append("Range limit: a bounded search; absence in this box is not a proof of impossibility.")
    return "\n".join(lines)

payload = eng_cubes()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Sums of Three Cubes")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: searching for three-cube representations of n up to 32 with coordinates up to 60, which does not establish the claim for all n")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

Full working program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_cubes(nmax=32, bound=60):
    cubes = {}
    for x in range(-bound, bound + 1):
        cubes[x ** 3] = x

    missing = []
    for n in range(1, nmax + 1):
        if n % 9 in (4, 5):
            lines.append("n=%d: impossible mod 9 (n = 4 or 5 mod 9)" % n)
            continue
        found = None
        for x in range(-bound, bound + 1):
            for y in range(-bound, bound + 1):
                z3 = n - x ** 3 - y ** 3
                if z3 in cubes:
                    found = (x, y, cubes[z3])
                    break
            if found:
                break
        if found:
            lines.append("n=%d: %d^3 + %d^3 + %d^3" % (n, found[0], found[1], found[2]))
        else:
            missing.append(n)
            lines.append("n=%d: no representation with |x|,|y|,|z| <= %d" % (n, bound))
    lines.append("Values with no small representation: %s" % str(missing))
    lines.append("Range limit: a bounded search; absence in this box is not a proof of impossibility.")
    return "\n".join(lines)

def main():
    print("Unsolved equation program: Sums of Three Cubes")
    print("Edition: public")
    print("STATUS: OPEN")
    print("Statement: For which integers n can n = x^3 + y^3 + z^3 with x, y, z integers? Are all n not congruent to 4 or 5 modulo 9 representable as a sum of three integer cubes?")
    payload = eng_cubes()
    print(payload)
    print("What this does NOT prove: searching for three-cube representations of n up to 32 with coordinates up to 60, which does not establish the claim for all n")
    print("Attempt summary: this public record carries no solving attempt; see the Signature mirror record.")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
